Stochastic Volterra Equations for Local Times of Spectrally Positive Lévy Processes with Gaussian Components
Wei Xu

Abstract
Following our previous work [ 68 ] , this paper continues to investigate the evolution dynamics of local times of spectrally positive Lévy processes with Gaussian components in the spatial direction. We prove that conditioned on the finiteness of the first time at which the local time at zero exceeds a given value, local times at positive line are equal in law to the unique solution of a stochastic Volterra equation driven by a Gaussian white noise and two Poisson random measures with convolution kernel given in terms of the scale function. Also, we obtain several equivalent stochastic equations by using the potential theoretic techniques and prove the strong existence and uniqueness by using the generalized Yamada-Watanabe theorems. Armed with the stochastic Volterra representation, we then establish a comparison principle for the local times of spectrally positive Lévy processes with various drifts or stopped when local times at zero exceed different given values, which proposes a stochastic flow enjoying the branching property. And also, we explore some novel properties of local times in the spatial direction including uniform moment estimates, ( 1 / 2 − ε ) (1/2-\varepsilon) -Hölder continuity and maximal inequality. By using the method of duality, we provide an exponential-affine representation of the Laplace functional in terms of the unique non-negative solution of a path-dependent nonlinear Volterra equation associated with the Laplace exponent of Lévy process. This gives another perspective on the evolution dynamics of local times in the spatial direction. MSC 2020 subject classifications: Primary 60G51, 60J55, 60H20; secondary 60G22, 60F17, 60G55 Keywords and phrases: Local time, Lévy process, stochastic Volterra equation, Ray-Knight theorem, comparison principle, Laplace functional.



1
Introduction and main results

As an important research branch in mathematics and probability, local times of Lévy processes have been deeply studied and also widely applied in various fields including random trees, queueing systems, ruin theory and so on; see
[
10
,
47
]
for a review.
More specifically, Aldous first introduced the celebrated Brownian continuum random tree as scaling limit of discrete random trees in
[
4
]
and reconstructed it in
[
5
]
from Brownian excursions whose local times are identical to the tree-width.
Further, Le Gall and Le Jan
[
51
,
52
]
coded the genealogy of general continuous-state branching processes via the exploration process defined by the local times at zero of the reflected processes of the time-reversed processes associated to a spectrally positive Lévy process with Laplace exponent being identically the branching mechanism.
Later, the well-known Lévy continuum random tree was constructed by Duquesne and Le Gall
[
22
]
from the spectrally positive Lévy process.
Furthermore, Duquesne proposed in a pioneer work
[
21
]
that each spectrally positive Lévy process stopped at hitting zero can be seen as the contour process of a compact real tree.
Therefore, more in-depth study of the local times of spectrally positive Lévy processes would contribute to understand the corresponding real trees.

Since the well-known
Ray-Knight theorems
were proved independently by Ray
[
61
]
and Knight
[
43
]
to connect Brownian local times with various Bessel processes, understanding the distributions and the inner structure of local times of symmetric Markov processes have motivated an abundance of amazing and wonderful work during the past decades, including Dynkin’s and Eisenbaum’s isomorphism theorems (see
[
23
,
25
]
), the alternate Ray-Knight theorems (see
[
24
,
27
,
64
]
), sufficient and necessary conditions for the Markov property (see
[
26
]
) and the joint continuity (see
[
57
,
58
]
). We refer to the monograph
[
59
]
for a survey on the local times of symmetric Markov processes.
Recently, the isomorphism theorems also have been established for non-symmetric Markov processes in
[
32
,
53
]
by using Markovian loop soups and permanental processes; see also
[
54
]
for details.
In particular, more explicit results have been obtained for the local times of Lévy processes with the symmetry being unnecessary, e.g., the joint continuity
[
9
]
, Hilbert transform
[
30
]
, Hölder regularity
[
8
]
, laws of the iterated logarithm
[
13
]
and so on.

In recent years, the inner structure and evolution dynamic of local times of spectrally positive Lévy processes in the spatial direction have attracted considerable attention because of their close connection to random trees and random maps.
Besides the Brownian case, the discontinuous trajectories of Lévy processes result in the fail of Markovianity and the intractability to their local times; see
[
26
]
.
Lambert
[
48
]
first used an excursion of compound Poisson process with unit negative drift and positive jumps to code a binary, splitting tree, and identified that local times of the excursion equal in distribution to a homogeneous, binary
Crump-Mode-Jagers branching process
.
Later, Lambert and Uribe Bravo
[
50
]
considered the totally ordered measured trees and coded them by the spectrally positive Lévy processes. Their results also illustrate the link between local times of spectrally positive Lévy processes and the corresponding tree-width processes.
Meanwhile, as the preceding compound Poisson processes converges weakly to a spectrally positive Lévy process after a suitable time-spatial scaling, Lambert and Simatos
[
49
]
proved the finite-dimensional convergence of their local times.
By marking each jumps of a driftless spectrally positive stable process by a random path, Forman et al.
[
33
]
established a locally uniform approximation for their local times and proved the finiteness of all moments of the Hölder coefficient.

In this work, we are mainly interested in the macro-evolution mechanism and sample path properties of local times of spectrally positive Lévy processes in the spatial direction, also known as
the second Ray-Knight theorems
.
In our previous work
[
68
]
, local times of spectrally positive stable processes stopped when the local time at zero exceeds a given value were intuitively described by the unique solution of a stochastic Volterra equation driven by Poisson random measures, which also gave a detailed account of the perturbations caused by each jump on local times.
In a recent work, Rivero and Contreras
[
63
]
also generalized the first and second Ray-Knight theorems for spectrally negative Lévy processes and establish several Poisson representations of their local times on the whole real line by using excursion theory.
As a continuation of
[
68
]
, in this paper we first establish a stochastic Volterra representation as well as a comparison principle for the local times of spectrally positive Lévy processes with Gaussian components, and then investigate their distribution properties as well as trajectory regularity.

1.1
Main results

Consider a spectrally positive Lévy process
\xi:=\{\xi(t):t\geq 0\}
defined on a filtrated probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathscr{F}_{t},\mathbf{P})
with a non-positive drift and a Gaussian component.
It can be fully characterized by the
Laplace exponent

\varPhi
that is of the form

\displaystyle\varPhi(\lambda):=\log\mathbf{E}\big[\exp\{-\lambda\xi(1)\}\big]=b\cdot\lambda+c\cdot\lambda^{2}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(e^{-\lambda y}-1+\lambda y\big)\,\nu(dy),\quad\lambda\geq 0,

(1.1)

for some
Lévy triplet

(b,c,\nu)
with
b\geq 0
,
c>0
and
\nu(dy)
being the Lévy measure on
(0,\infty)
satisfying

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(y\wedge y^{2}\big)\,\nu(dy)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1\wedge(2y)\big)\,\bar{\nu}(y)\,dy<\infty,

(1.2)

where
\bar{\nu}(y):=\nu([y,\infty))
is the
tail-function
of
\nu(dy)
.
The function
\varPhi
is zero at zero and increases strictly to infinity at infinity.
It is strictly convex and infinitely differentiable on
(0,\infty)
with

\varPhi^{\,\prime}(0)=-\mathbf{E}\big[\xi(1)\big]=b
.
The process
\xi
drifts to
-\infty
or is recurrent according as
b>0
or
=0
.

For every
t\geq 0
, let
\mu_{\xi,t}(dy)
be the
occupation measure
of
\xi
on the time interval
[0,t]
given for every non-negative and measurable function
f
on
\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}f\big(\xi(s)\big)\,ds\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\,\mu_{\xi,t}(dx).

(1.3)

Theorem 1 in
[
14
, p.126]
induces that the random measure
\mu_{\xi,t}(dx)
is absolutely continuous almost surely with respect to the Lebesgue measure and the density, denoted by
\{L_{\xi}(x,t):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
, is square integrable.
The identity (
1.3
) turns into the well-known
occupation density formula

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}f\big(\xi(r)\big)\,dr\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)L_{\xi}(x,t)\,dx,\quad t\geq 0.

(1.4)

The existence of Gaussian component yields that
\varPhi(\lambda)=O(\lambda^{2})
as
\lambda\to\infty
and

\int_{1}^{\infty}1/\varPhi(\lambda)\,d\lambda<\infty
,
which along with Lemma 2.2 in
[
49
]
induces that the two-parameter process

L_{\xi}:=\{L_{\xi}(x,t):x\in\mathbb{R},t\geq 0\}
is jointly continuous almost surely.

In particular, the process
\{L_{\xi}(0,t):t\geq 0\}
is continuous and non-decreasing with
L_{\xi}(0,\infty)\in(0,\infty]
a.s.
This allows us to define the
inverse local time

\tau_{\xi}^{L}:=\{\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta):\zeta\geq 0\}
at level
0
by

\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta)=\infty
if
\zeta>L_{\xi}(0,\infty)
and

\displaystyle\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta):=\inf\big\{s\geq 0:L_{\xi}(0,s)\geq\zeta\big\},\quad\mbox{if }\zeta\in\big[0,L_{\xi}(0,\infty)\big].

(1.5)

For any
\zeta>0
, the transience and recurrence of
\xi
induce that
L_{\xi}(0,\infty)=\infty
and
\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta)<\infty
a.s. when
b=0
or
L_{\xi}(0,\infty)<\infty
a.s. and
\mathbf{P}(\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta)=\infty)>0
when
b>0
.
In this work, we are mainly interested in the evolution dynamic of local times in the spatial direction conditioned on the finiteness of the first time at which the local time at zero exceeds a given value, i.e.,

L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
is the process
\big\{L_{\xi}(x,\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta)):x\geq 0\big\}
conditioned on
\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta)<\infty
.

Our first main result establishes a stochastic Volterra equation driven by a Gaussian white noise and two Poisson random measures for
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
with integrands being in terms of the
scale function

W:=\{W(x):x\in\mathbb{R}\}
associated to
\varPhi
.
The function
W
is a non-negative function that is identically zero on
(-\infty,0)
and characterized on
[0,\infty)
as a continuous, strictly increasing function with Laplace transform

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda x}W(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{\mathit{\Phi}(\lambda)},\quad\lambda>0;

(1.6)

see
[
18
,
46
,
47
]
for details.
It is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
and twice continuously differentiable on
(0,\infty)
with

\displaystyle W^{\prime}(0)=\frac{1}{c},\qquad\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}W^{\prime}(x)\leq\frac{1}{c}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad W(\infty)=\begin{cases}\infty,&\mbox{if $b=0$};\\
1/b,&\mbox{if $b>0$}.\end{cases}

(1.7)

This uniform upper bound comes from Corollary
4.6
.
The next theorem will be proved in Section
4
.

Theorem 1.1

For each
\zeta\geq 0
, the process
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
equals in distribution to the unique non-negative continuous solution of the stochastic Volterra equation

\displaystyle X_{\zeta}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot c\cdot W^{\prime}(t)+\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t)-W(t-y)\big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}W^{\prime}(t-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

(1.9)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t-s)-W(t-s-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy),\quad t\geq 0,

where
N_{0}(dz,dy)
is a Poisson random measure on
(0,\infty)^{2}
with intensity
\bar{\nu}(y)\,dz\,dy
,
B_{c}(ds,dz)
is a Gaussian white noise on
(0,\infty)^{2}
with intensity
2c\cdot ds\,dz
and
\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)
is a compensated Poisson random measure on
(0,\infty)^{3}
with intensity
ds\,dz\,\nu(dy)
.

Definitions of
W
and
(N_{0},B_{c},\widetilde{N}_{\nu})
tell that the stochastic Volterra equation (
1.9
) is fully determined by the
characteristic vector

(\zeta\,;\,b,c,\nu)
.
The two upper bounds in (
1.2
) and (
1.7
) induce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t)-W(t-y)\big)\,\bar{\nu}(y)\,dy+\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t)-W(t-y)\big)^{2}\,\nu(dy)\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\int_{0}^{\infty}(t\wedge y)^{2}\,\nu(dy)<\infty,

(1.10)

uniformly in
t
on compacts.
This along with (
1.7
) allows us to consider the three stochastic integrals in (
1.9
) as Itô’s integrals that have been deeply explored in
[
39
,
67
]
.
Hence it is natural to formulate the definition of solutions to (
1.9
) by extending those of stochastic differential equations in
[
39
, Chapter IV.1]
and
[
44
]
.
More specifically, by a
solution
of (
1.9
), we mean a process
X_{\zeta}\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R}_{+})
defined on a filtrated probability space on which three mutually independent driving noises
(N_{0},B_{c},\widetilde{N}_{\nu})
as in Theorem
1.1
are defined such that (
1.9
) holds almost surely.
The
uniqueness
is said to hold for (
1.9
) if any two solutions equal in distribution.
Also, we recall the quadruple
(X_{\zeta},N_{0},B_{c},\widetilde{N}_{\nu})
a solution of (
1.9
) to emphasize the particular role of driving noises.

Remark 1.2

In the Brownian case, i.e.,
\nu(\mathbb{R}_{+})=0
,
we have
W^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\{-\frac{b}{c}\cdot x\}
.
By the equality
e^{-\frac{b}{c}\cdot(t-s)}=1-\int_{s}^{t}e^{-\frac{b}{c}\cdot(r-s)}\,dr
and the stochastic Fubini’s theorem; see (
2.8
), we can write (
1.9
) as

\displaystyle X_{\zeta}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{b}{c}\cdot X_{\zeta}(s)\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\frac{1}{c}\,B_{c}(ds,dz),\quad t\geq 0,

(1.11)

The strong non-negative continuous solution uniquely exists and is a branching diffusion starting with
\zeta
ancestors. In particular, when
b=0
and
c=1/2
, it is a square of
0
-dimensional Bessel process and (
1.11
) is identical to (1.4) in
[
1
]
with
\mu=1
.

Usually, the last two stochastic Volterra integrals in (
1.9
) are not local martingales but have mean zero.
Taking expectations on both sides of (
1.9
) and then using Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[L^{\xi}_{\zeta}(x)\big]=\mathbf{E}\big[X_{\zeta}(x)\big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot c\cdot W^{\prime}(x)+\zeta\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(x)-W(x-y)\big)\bar{\nu}(y)\,dy

(1.12)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot c\cdot W^{\prime}(x)+\zeta\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}W^{\prime}(x-y)\bar{\bar{\nu}}(y)\,dy,

(1.13)

where
\bar{\bar{\nu}}(x):=\int_{[x,\infty)}\bar{\nu}(y)\,dy
is the integrated tail-function of
\bar{\nu}
.
Furthermore, applying the identity in Lemma
2.1
to (
1.12
) induces that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[L^{\xi}_{\zeta}(x)\big]=\mathbf{E}\big[X_{\zeta}(x)\big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot\big(1-b\cdot W(x)\big)\in[0,\zeta],

(1.14)

which is identically
\zeta
when
b=0
or decreases strictly to
0
as
x\to\infty
when
b>0
.
The finite height of the process
\xi
stopped at
\tau^{\xi}_{\zeta}
induces that
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}(x)\to 0
a.s. as
x\to\infty
and the point
0
is a cemetery state.
Analogue to the criticality for branching processes, we say
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
is
critical
if
b=0
or
subcritical
if
b>0
.

Remark 1.3

With the help of (
1.12
) and (
1.14
), we merge the first term on the right side of (
1.9
) with the compensator of the second term to obtain the following alternate representation

\displaystyle X_{\zeta}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot\big(1-b\cdot W(x)\big)+\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t)-W(t-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{0}(dz,dy)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}W^{\prime}(t-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

(1.16)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t-s)-W(t-s-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy),\quad t\geq 0,

with
\widetilde{N}_{0}(dz,dy):=N_{0}(dz,dy)-\bar{\nu}(y)\,dz\,dy
.
Additionally,
by Theorem 7.1 in
[
39
, p.84]
one can find a Brownian motion
B
on an extension of the original probability space such that almost surely

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}W^{\prime}(t-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)=\int_{0}^{t}W^{\prime}(t-s)\sqrt{2c\cdot X_{\zeta}(s)}\,dB(s),\quad t\geq 0.

Figure 1.1:
A sample path of spectrally positive Lévy processes containing three typical kinds of excursions away from level
x
: (i)
\mathcal{E}_{+}^{x}
excursions that are completely above
x
(the green trajectories); (ii)
\mathcal{E}_{-}^{x}
excursions that are completely blow
x
(the blue trajectory); (iii)
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{x}
excursions that move below
x
until jumping into
(x,\infty)
and then stay above
x
up to hitting
x
(the magenta trajectory).

By using results in Chapter IV in
[
14
]
and the spatial homogeneity, the local times of
\xi
at any level
x
(up to a constant multiplicative factor) also can be defined by approximations involving the numbers of excursions away from
x
with intervals of certain types.
Because of the existence of Gaussian component and the lake of negative jumps, excursions away from
x
can classified into three distinguishable types: (i)
\mathcal{E}_{+}^{x}
consists of all excursions that are completely above
x
; (ii)
\mathcal{E}_{-}^{x}
consists of all excursions that are completely blow
x
; (iii)
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{x}
consists of all excursions that move below
x
until jumping into
(x,\infty)
and then stay above
x
up to hitting
x
; see Figure
1.1
.
We refer to
[
63
]
for more details.
Roughly speaking, the first two types come from the Gaussian component and the third type results from the positive jumps.
Our proofs further elaborate on their various contributions to the local times at different levels and the equation (
1.9
).
More precisely, excursions
\mathcal{E}_{-}^{0}
contribute only to the local times of negative levels and hence have no connection to (
1.9
).
In contrast, excursions
\mathcal{E}_{+}^{0}
contribute only to the local times of positive levels as well as three of the four terms on the right side of (
1.9
) in different ways, i.e., the first term on the right side of (
1.9
) describe their average contribution to the local times at different levels; meanwhile, perturbations of their sub-excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{-}^{x}\cup\mathcal{E}_{+}^{x}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{x}
respectively compose part of the last two stochastic Volterra integrals in (
1.9
).
Different from the previous two types, excursions
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{0}
contribute to the local times of both positive and negative levels.
Their average contribution at positive levels along with the randomness of their overshoots composes the first stochastic Volterra integral in (
1.9
).
Also, perturbations of their sub-excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{-}^{x}\cup\mathcal{E}_{+}^{x}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{x}
compose the rest part of the last two stochastic Volterra integrals in (
1.9
), respectively.

Remark 1.4

In view of (
1.7
) and (
1.10
) the first two terms on the right side of (
1.9
) converge respectively to
\zeta
and
0
as
t\to 0+
, which indicates that excursions
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{0}
make no contribution to the local times at level
0
. This along with the spatial homogeneity asserts that local times at any level
x
are fully determined by excursions
\mathcal{E}_{-}^{x}\cup\mathcal{E}_{+}^{x}
.

The monotonicity of
L_{\xi}
in the time variable induces that when
b=0
, both
\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta)
and
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
increase almost surely in
\zeta
.
However, this property fails when
b>0
, since the definition of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
replies on the conditional law
\mathbf{P}(\cdot\,|\,\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta)<\infty)
.
On the other hand, the large negative drift will pull
\xi
back into negative line quickly.
Hence, it is natural to conjecture that local times
L_{\xi}
would decrease as
b
increases.
As the second main result, these observations motivate us to establish in the next theorem a comparison principle for local times of spectrally positive Lévy processes with different drifts or stopped when local times at zero exceed different given values.
The proof can be found in Section
4
.

Theorem 1.5

For
b_{1},b_{2},\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}\geq 0
and
i=1,2
, let
\xi_{i}
be a spectrally positive Lévy process with Lévy triplet
(b_{i},c,\nu)
.
There exists a filtrated probability space endowed with two processes
X_{\zeta_{1}}^{b_{1}},X_{\zeta_{2}}^{b_{2}}\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R}_{+})
and three driving noises
(N_{0},B_{c},\widetilde{N}_{\nu})
defined as in Theorem
1.1
such that the following two claims hold.

(1)

For
i=1,2
, the process
L^{\xi_{i}}_{\zeta_{i}}
equals in distribution to
X_{\zeta_{i}}^{b_{i}}
and the quadruple
(X_{\zeta_{i}}^{b_{i}},N_{0},B_{c},\widetilde{N}_{\nu})
is the unique solution of (
1.9
) with characteristic vector
(\zeta_{i}\,;\,b_{i},c,\nu)
.

(2)

If
b_{1}\geq b_{2}
and
\zeta_{1}\leq\zeta_{2}
, we have
\mathbf{P}\big(X_{\zeta_{1}}^{b_{1}}(t)\leq X_{\zeta_{2}}^{b_{2}}(t),t\geq 0\big)=1
.

Comparison principles have been widely established for classic deterministic/stochastic differential equations (see
[
36
,
39
,
65
]
) and ordinary Volterra equations (see
[
16
,
35
]
).
However, by comparison, much less is known for stochastic Volterra equations, e.g., a comparison principle was first obtained in
[
66
]
with only Volterra drift and then generalized to the general case in
[
17
,
29
]
under some regular conditions on the kernel and coefficients.
To the best of our knowledge, all results in the existing literature do not apply to our stochastic equation (
1.9
).

Compared to the comparison principles in the aforementioned references for differential equations with explicit, manifest initial states and drifts, the equation (
1.9
) not only has a random and time-dependent initial state, it also seems to have no drift.
This makes our comparison principle puzzling and incomprehensible.
In order to make it more understandable in intuition and integrate it into the classic theory, in the next theorem we provide an equivalent representation of (
1.9
) by separating the impact of drift
b
on the local times from that of diffusion and jumps.
Consider the function

\displaystyle\varPhi_{0}(\lambda):=\varPhi(\lambda)-b\cdot\lambda,\quad\lambda\geq 0,

(1.17)

which is the Laplace exponent of a driftless spectrally positive Lévy process with triplet
(0,c,\nu)
.
Denote by
W_{0}
and
W^{\prime}_{0}
the scale function associated to
\varPhi_{0}
and its right-derivative respectively.

Theorem 1.6

The stochastic Volterra equation (
1.9
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle X_{\zeta}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot c\cdot W^{\prime}_{0}(t)+\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W_{0}(t)-W_{0}(t-y)\big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)

(1.20)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}b\cdot W^{\prime}_{0}(t-s)X_{\zeta}(s)ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}W^{\prime}_{0}(t-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W_{0}(t-s)-W_{0}(t-s-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy).\quad t\geq 0.

Similarly as in Remark
1.3
, one can use the identity in Lemma
2.1
with
b=0
to combine the first term on right side of (
1.20
) together with the compensator of the second term and then write (
1.20
) as

\displaystyle X_{\zeta}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta+\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W_{0}(t)-W_{0}(t-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{0}(dz,dy)

(1.23)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}b\cdot W^{\prime}_{0}(t-s)X_{\zeta}(s)ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}W^{\prime}_{0}(t-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W_{0}(t-s)-W_{0}(t-s-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy),\quad t\geq 0.

Recall that the scale function
W_{0}
is independent of the drift parameter
b
.
The time-independent initial state and non-positive drift make it natural to expect comparison principles for (
1.23
) and then (
1.9
).

Analogous to the stochastic dominance between random variables, we say the process
Y_{1}\in D(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
is
absolutely dominant over

Y_{2}\in D(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
, denoted as
Y_{1}\geq Y_{2}
, if
\mathbf{P}(Y_{1}(t)\geq Y_{2}(t),t\geq 0)=1
.
Moreover,
Y_{1}
is said to be
first-order stochastically dominant over

Y_{2}
, denoted as
Y_{1}\succeq Y_{2}
, if

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}Y_{1}(s)\mu(ds)\geq x\Big)\geq\mathbf{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}Y_{2}(s)\mu(ds)\geq x\Big),\quad x\in\mathbb{R},

for any measure
\mu(ds)
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
A family of processes
\{Y_{\zeta}\}_{\zeta\geq 0}
is said to be
absolutely increasing
or
stochastically increasing
[resp.
absolutely decreasing
or
stochastically decreasing
] if
Y_{\zeta_{2}}\geq Y_{\zeta_{1}}
or
Y_{\zeta_{2}}\succeq Y_{\zeta_{1}}
[resp.
Y_{\zeta_{1}}\geq Y_{\zeta_{2}}
or
Y_{\zeta_{1}}\succeq Y_{\zeta_{2}}
] when
\zeta_{2}\geq\zeta_{1}
.

Our comparison principle claims that the local time processes
\{L^{\xi}_{\zeta}\}_{\zeta\geq 0}
are absolutely increasing if
b=0
or stochastically increasing if
b>0
.
This indicates that a class of stochastic flows are hidden in the local times
L_{\xi}
.
Recently, Contreras and Xu
[
19
]
reconstructed them and investigated their (conditionally) asymptotic properties by using excursion theory and fluctuation theory of Lévy processes.
In the past decades, various stochastic flows have been derived from Lévy processes and their related models. For instance, Ray-Knight theorems for (reflected) Brownian motions introduce the flows of squared Bessel processes, Their pathwise construction was recently given by Aïdékon et al
[
2
]
, in which a flow called
Jacobi flow
was also defined by strong solutions of stochastic differential equations that were constructed via a perturbed reflecting Brownian motion.
For a general spectrally positive Lévy process,
Ray-Knight theorems in
[
22
]
states that local times of the reflected processes of its time-reversed processes at
0
equal in law to the flows of continuous-state branching processes, which were reconstructed in
[
20
]
as strong solutions of stochastic differential equations driven by a Gaussian white noise and a Poisson random measure.

Without the existence and uniqueness of strong solution, one cannot construct the stochastic flows in
L_{\xi}
by using (
1.9
) as in the aforementioned literature.
Here the
strong solution
of (
1.9
) is defined as a solution
(X_{\zeta},N_{0},B_{c},\widetilde{N}_{\nu})
with
X_{\zeta}
being equal almost surely to a Borel measurable function of
(N_{0},B_{c},\widetilde{N}_{\nu})
.
Although the scale function
W
is twice continuously differentiable
on
(0,\infty)
, the second derivative
W^{\prime\prime}
may be singular around
0
, i.e. Theorem 3.1 in
[
12
]
shows that

\displaystyle W^{\prime\prime}(0+)=\frac{b-\bar{\bar{\nu}}(0+)}{c^{2}},

which is finite if and only if
\bar{\bar{\nu}}(0+)<\infty
that is equivalent to
\int_{0}^{\infty}y\,\nu(dy)<\infty
.
Moreover, note that all coefficients in (
1.9
) are
1/2
-Hölder continuous.
Consequently, we cannot obtain the strong solutions by using the standard Picard iteration and the Euler method as usual.
Fortunately, in the case of
\bar{\bar{\nu}}(0+)<\infty
, the function
W^{\prime\prime}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and also differentiable on
(0,\infty)
with derivative
W^{\prime\prime\prime}
enjoying the regularity of
\bar{\nu}
; see Theorem 2 in
[
18
]
.
This allows us to improve the method developed in
[
6
,
60
]
to prove the pathwise uniqueness for (
1.9
) and then establish the strong existence and uniqueness by using the generalized Yamada-Watanabe theorems in
[
69
]
; see the next theorem whose the proof is given in Section
5
.

Theorem 1.7

If
\bar{\bar{\nu}}(0+)<\infty
, the stochastic Volterra equation (
1.9
) has a unique strong solution. Moreover, the solution is a semimartingale with the following representation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{split}X_{\zeta}(t)&=\zeta\cdot c\cdot W^{\prime}(t)+\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t)-W(t-y)\big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)\cr&\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\Big(W^{\prime\prime}(0+)\cdot M(s)+\int_{0}^{s}W^{\prime\prime\prime}(s-r)M(r)\,dr\Big)\,ds+\frac{1}{c}\cdot M(t),\cr M(t)&=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{r}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{r-s}^{\infty}\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)\,dr+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\,B_{c}(ds,dz).\end{split}\right.

(1.24)

Armed with the stochastic Volterra equation (
1.9
), we are allowed to use the instruments and methods provided by modern probability theory and stochastic analysis to revisit or refine the well-known results for local times of Lévy processes and also investigate their unknown properties.
The main contribution in the second part of this work
is to illustrate the strength of (
1.9
) by using them to study the distribution properties and sample path regularity of local times in the spatial direction.
As the first result, in the next theorem we provide a uniform upper bound for all moments of local times of any levels.

Theorem 1.8

For each
p\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
\zeta,x\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|L^{\xi}_{\zeta}(x)\big|^{p}\Big]\leq C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot\big(1+W(x)\big)^{2p-2}.

(1.25)

In the critical case (
b=0
), the uniform upper bound in (
1.25
) can be changed into
C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot(1+x)^{2p-2}
since
W(x)\leq x/c
; see (
1.7
).
Differently, when
b>0
, the third claim in (
1.7
) induces that it can be bounded by
C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})
uniformly in
x\geq 0
.
Moreover, it should be stressed that our uniform upper bound is far from being optimal and can be further improved if some
L^{p}
-estimates are provided for
W^{\prime\prime}
, e.g., for a drifted Brownian, in view of Remark
1.2
we can replace the upper bound by
c_{1}\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot\exp\{-c_{2}\cdot x\}
for some constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
.

The second property we are interested in is the Hölder regularity of the trajectories of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
. Finding condition for the joint continuity of the local times motivated a large number of magnificent work in the last century; see
[
14
, Chapter V.5]
for a survey.
For instance, the joint Hölder continuity of local times of
\alpha
-stable Lévy processes with
\alpha\in(1,2]
was first obtained in
[
15
]
by discretizing the occupation measures. The optimal Hölder exponent was given in
[
8
]
and the finiteness of all moments of the Hölder coefficient in the driftless case was proved in the recent work
[
33
]
.
In the general case, local times in the spatial direction were proved to be Hölder continuous in
[
9
,
11
]
if the reciprocal of characteristic function has real part decreasing at infinity as a power function.

In the next theorem, we prove the Hölder continuity of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
by using the inequalities of stochastic integral to (
1.9
) and then the Kolmogorov continuity theorem.
Moreover, we also provide a uniform upper bound for all moments of the Hölder coefficients and the local maximal of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
by using the Garsia-Rodemich-Rumsey inequality.
For
\kappa\in(0,1]
and
x>0
, the
\kappa
-Hölder coefficient
of a Hölder continuous function
f
on
[0,x]
is defined by

\displaystyle\big\|f\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{x}}:=\sup_{0\leq y<z\leq x}\frac{|f(y)-f(z)|}{|y-z|^{\kappa}}.

Theorem 1.9

The process
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
is locally Hölder continuous with index strictly less than
1/2
.
Moreover, for each
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
and
p\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
\zeta,x\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big\|L^{\xi}_{\zeta}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{x}}^{p}\Big]\leq C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot(1+x)^{p(3-\kappa)}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbf{E}\Big[\sup_{z\in[0,x]}\big|L^{\xi}_{\zeta}(z)\big|^{p}\Big]\leq C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot(1+x)^{3p}.

(1.26)

Compared to the
(\frac{\alpha-1}{2}-\varepsilon)
-Hölder continuity in the
\alpha
-stable case; see Theorem 2.9 in
[
68
]
, the Gaussian component significantly improves the regularity of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
even though more perturbations are added.
Actually, this is not surprising and inexplicable, since the real part the reciprocal of characteristic function is
O(|z|^{-2})
at infinity and hence
L_{\xi}
is
(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)
-Hölder continuous in the spatial variable; see
[
9
,
11
]
and
[
14
, Exercise 4, p.151]
.
As we mentioned in Remark
1.4
, similarly to Brownian local times, local times at level
x
are only contributed by excursions
\mathcal{E}^{x}_{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{x}_{-}
and hence should enjoy the same regularity.

The last property of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
we explore in this work is its Laplace functional.
In contrast to Brownian local times whose the Laplace transform can be given as an exponential affine function of the initial state and the solution of a Riccati equation by using their branching property,
the evolution dynamic of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
is much intractable because of the lack of Markovanity.
Fortunately, an intuitive comparison between the stochastic integrals in (
1.9
) and (
1.11
) reveals that
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
should enjoy the analogous “affine property” of Brownian local times.
By generalizing the duality method in
[
68
]
, we establish in the next theorem an explicit representation of Laplace functionals

\mathbf{E}\big[\exp\{-\int_{[0,x]}L^{\xi}_{\zeta}(x-s)\,\mu(ds)\}\big]
in term of the unique solution of the
path-dependent nonlinear Volterra equation

\displaystyle V_{\mu}(t)=\int_{[0,t]}W^{\prime}(t-s)\,\mu(ds)-\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(s)W^{\prime}(t-s)\,ds,\quad t\geq 0,

(1.27)

where
\mu(ds)
is a
\sigma
-finite measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}
is a nonlinear operator acting on a locally bounded
function
f
by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ f(t):=c\cdot\big(f(t)\big)^{2}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\exp\Big\{-\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}f(r)\,dr\Big\}-1+\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}f(r)\,dr\bigg)\nu(dy).

(1.28)

Theorem 1.10

For each
\sigma
-finite measure
\mu(ds)
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
the nonlinear Volterra equation (
1.27
) has a unique global solution
V_{\mu}\in D(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R}_{+})
and the Laplace functional of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
admits the representation

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\bigg[\exp\Big\{-\int_{[0,x]}L^{\xi}_{\zeta}(x-s)\,\mu(ds)\Big\}\bigg]=\exp\big\{-\zeta\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}\circ V_{\mu}(x)\big\},\quad x\geq 0,

(1.29)

where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}
is a nonlinear operator acting on a locally bounded
function
f
by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}\circ f(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle c\cdot f(t)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(1-\exp\Big\{-\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}f(r)\,dr\Big\}\bigg)\,\bar{\nu}(y)\,dy,\quad t\geq 0.

(1.30)

It is worth mentioning that Rivero and Contreras
[
63
]
used the excursion theory to provide a different representation for the Laplace functional of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
as follows

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\bigg[\exp\Big\{-\int_{[0,x]}L^{\xi}_{\zeta}(x-s)\,\mu(ds)\Big\}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\exp\bigg\{-\zeta\cdot\int_{D_{0}}\Big(1-\exp\Big\{-\int_{[0,x]}\ell_{\mathbf{e}}(x-s)\,\mu(ds)\Big\}\Big)\boldsymbol{n}_{0}(d\mathbf{e})

\displaystyle\quad-\zeta\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(1-\exp\Big\{-\int_{0}^{y}dr\int_{D_{0}}\Big(1-\exp\Big\{-\int_{[r,x]}\ell_{\mathbf{e}}(x-s)\,\mu(ds)\Big\}\Big)\underline{\boldsymbol{n}}(d\mathbf{e})\Big\}\Big)\bar{\nu}(y)dy\bigg\},

where
\ell_{\mathbf{e}}(s)
is the total local times of excursion
\mathbf{e}
at level
s
,
\boldsymbol{n}_{0}
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{n}}
are the measures of excursions away from
0
for
\xi
and its reflected process at infimum respectively.
Intuitively, the two terms in the Laplace exponent should be identical to the corresponding terms in (
1.29
)-(
1.30
).
Due to our limited acknowledge of excursion theory, the equivalence between these two representations remains to be addressed.

Finally, we end this work with some discussion of the total local times in the subcritical case (
b>0
).
Proposition 4 in
[
14
, p.130]
tells that the process
\tau_{\xi}^{L}
is a subordinator killed at an independent exponential time and its Laplace transform is of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[\exp\big\{-\lambda\cdot\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta)\big\}\big]=\exp\big\{-\zeta/u^{\lambda}(0)\big\},\quad\lambda,\,\zeta\geq 0,

(1.31)

where
u^{\lambda}:=\{u^{\lambda}(y):y\in\mathbb{R}\}
is the density of the
\lambda
-resolvent kernel
of
\xi
.
This induces that

\mathbf{P}\big(L_{\xi}(0,\infty)\geq\zeta\big)=1-\mathbf{P}\big(\tau_{\xi}^{L}(\zeta)=\infty\big)=\exp\big\{-\zeta/u^{0}(0)\big\}

and hence
L_{\xi}(0,\infty)
is exponentially distributed with mean
u^{0}(0)
.
Define the process

\displaystyle L^{\xi}_{\infty}:=\big\{L_{\xi}(x,\infty):x\geq 0\big\}\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\big\{L_{\xi}\big(x,\tau_{\xi}^{L}(L_{\xi}(0,\infty))\big):x\geq 0\big\}.

(1.32)

All preceding results and their proofs can be generalized to
L^{\xi}_{\infty}
with the constant
\zeta
replaced by an exponentially distributed random variable
\varrho
with mean
u^{0}(0)
; see the next corollary as one example.

Corollary 1.11

When
b>0
, the process
L_{\infty}^{\xi}
equals in distribution to the unique continuous solution of (
1.9
) with
\zeta=\varrho
.
For any
\sigma
-finite measure
\mu(ds)
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, it has Laplace functional

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\bigg[\exp\Big\{-\int_{[0,x]}L_{\infty}^{\xi}(x-s)\,\mu(ds)\Big\}\bigg]=\Big(1+u^{0}(0)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}\circ V_{\mu}(x)\Big)^{-1},\quad x\geq 0.

(1.33)

Organization of this paper.
In Section
2
, we provide some auxiliary results for scale functions and stochastic Volterra integrals.
In Section
3
, we recall two stochastic Volterra representations for the local times of compound Poisson processes and then show that after a suitable scaling, they behave like those of the desired Lévy process.
In Section
4
, we first establish the stochastic Volterra representation for the process
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
by proving that the stochastic Volterra equation solved by the local times of compound Poisson processes converges weakly to (
1.9
).
Thereafter, we prove the comparison principle with the help of our weak convergence arguments.
The existence and uniqueness of strong solution are proved in Section
5
.
Section
6
is devoted to establish the moment estimates given in Theorem
1.8
and the general equivalent representation of (
1.9
) that will induces Theorem
1.6
as a by-product.
Theorem
1.9
is proved in Section
7
.
The well-posedness of nonliear Volterra equation (
1.27
) and the affine representation of the Laplace functional of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
are established in Section
8
, and also, the uniqueness in law of solution to (
1.9
) is proved as a corollary.
In Appendix
A
, we recall some basic theory of stochastic integrals driven by semimartingales indexed by a Banach space.

Notation.
Let
\mathbb{N}
be the space of all natural numbers including
0
.
For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let
x^{+}:=x\vee 0
,
x^{-}:=x\wedge 0
and
[x]
be the integer part of
x
.
Let
\overset{\rm f.d.d.}{\longrightarrow}
,
\overset{\rm u.c.}{\to}
,
\overset{\rm a.s.}{\to}
,
\overset{\rm d}{\to}
and
\overset{\rm p}{\to}
be the convergence in the sense of finite dimensional distributions, the uniform convergence on compacts, almost sure convergence, convergence in distribution and convergence in probability respectively.
We also use
\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}
,
\overset{\rm d}{=}
and
\overset{\rm p}{=}
to denote almost sure equality, equality in distribution and equality in probability respectively.

For a Banach space
\mathbb{V}
with a norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{V}}
, let
D([0,\infty),\mathbb{V})
be the space of all càdlàg
\mathbb{V}
-valued functions endowed with the Skorokhod topology and
C([0,\infty),\mathbb{V})
the space of all continuous
\mathbb{V}
-valued functions endowed with the uniform topology.
For any
\mathcal{T}\subset[0,\infty)
and
p\in(0,\infty]
, let
L^{p}(\mathcal{T};\mathbb{V})
be the space of all
\mathbb{V}
-valued measurable functions
f
on
\mathcal{T}
satisfying

\big\|f\big\|_{L^{p}_{\mathcal{T}}}^{p}:=\int_{\mathcal{T}}\big\|f(x)\big\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{p}dx<\infty.

We also write
\|f\|_{L^{p}_{T}}
for
\|f\|_{L^{p}_{[0,T]}}
and
\|f\|_{L^{{}^{p}}}
for
\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\infty}}
.
Let
L^{p}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{V}):=\cap_{T\geq 0}L^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{V})
.
For two functions
f,g
and a
\sigma
-finite measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
the two convolutions
f*g
and
f*d\mu
are defined by

\displaystyle f*g(x):=\int_{0}^{x}f(x-y)g(y)\,dy\quad\mbox{and}\quad f*d\mu(x):=\int_{[0,x]}f(x-y)\,\mu(dy)\quad x\geq 0.

We use
C
to denote a positive constant whose value might change from line to line.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we first provide some auxiliary results for scale functions and then give some moment estimates and stochastic Fubini’s theorems for stochastic Volterra integrals with respect to Gaussian white noise and Poisson random measure.

2.1
Scale functions

Associated to the functions
\bar{\nu}
and
\bar{\bar{\nu}}
we recall two alternate representations of the Laplace exponent
\varPhi

\displaystyle\varPhi(\lambda)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle b\cdot\lambda+c\cdot\lambda^{2}+\lambda\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1-e^{-\lambda x}\big)\bar{\nu}(x)\,dx

(2.1)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle b\cdot\lambda+c\cdot\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda x}\,\bar{\bar{\nu}}(x)\,dx,

(2.2)

which appear frequently in
[
14
,
47
]
and can be obtained by using Fubini’s theorem to (
1.1
).
Using integration by parts to (
1.6
) gives that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda x}W^{\prime}(x)\,dx=\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}W(x)\,dx=\frac{\lambda}{\varPhi(\lambda)},\quad\lambda>0,

(2.3)

which also holds for all

\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}:=\{x+\mathtt{i}z\,:\,x\geq 0\mbox{ and }z\in\mathbb{R}\}

if
b>0
and all

\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
if
b=0
; see the proof of Theorem 2.1 in
[
46
]
.
We first provide an identity that has been used to derive (
1.14
).

Lemma 2.1

For all
x\geq 0
, we have
b\cdot W(x)+c\cdot W^{\prime}(x)+W^{\prime}*\bar{\bar{\nu}}(x)=1
.

Proof.
To prove the identity, we define a
\sigma
-finite measure
\mu_{b}(dy):=c\cdot\delta_{0}(dy)+\big(b+\bar{\bar{\nu}}(y)\big)\,dy
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}

with
\delta_{0}(dy)
being the Dirac measure at point
0
.
It has Laplace transform

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{-\lambda y}\,\mu_{b}(dy)=\frac{b}{\lambda}+c+\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda x}\bar{\bar{\nu}}(x)\,dx=\frac{\varPhi(\lambda)}{\lambda^{2}}.

Here the second equality follows from (
2.1
).
This along with (
2.3
) induces that for
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{-\lambda x}W^{\prime}*d\mu_{b}(x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{-\lambda x}W^{\prime}(x)\,dx\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{-\lambda y}\,\mu_{b}(dy)=\frac{1}{\lambda},

which identifies that
W^{\prime}*d\mu_{b}(x)=1
for almost every
x\geq 0
.
Additionally, note that

\displaystyle W^{\prime}*d\mu_{b}(x)=b\cdot W(x)+c\cdot W^{\prime}(x)+W^{\prime}*\bar{\bar{\nu}}(x),

which tells that
W^{\prime}*d\mu_{b}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and hence
W^{\prime}*d\mu_{b}(x)=1
for all
x\geq 0
.

\Box

Lemma 2.2

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cap(-b,\infty)
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|e^{-\lambda(x+\delta)}W^{\prime}(x+\delta)-e^{-\lambda x}W^{\prime}(x)\big|^{2}\,dx\leq C\cdot\delta.

Proof.
Note that
\varPhi(\mathtt{i}z+\lambda)\sim b\lambda
as
|z|\to 0
and is
O(|z|^{2})
as
|z|\to\infty
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-(\mathrm{i}z+\lambda)x}W^{\prime}(x)\,dx\bigg|=\bigg|\frac{\mathtt{i}z+\lambda}{\varPhi(\mathtt{i}z+\lambda)}\bigg|\leq C\cdot\Big(1\wedge\frac{1}{|z|}\Big),\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.4)

By using the Fourier isometry along with the square integrability of
e^{-\lambda x}W^{\prime}(x)
and then the change of variables,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big|e^{-\lambda(x+\delta)}W^{\prime}(x+\delta)-e^{-\lambda x}W^{\prime}(x)\Big|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\mathtt{i}zx-\lambda(x+\delta)}W^{\prime}(x+\delta)\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\mathtt{i}zx-\lambda x}W^{\prime}(x)\,dx\bigg|^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big|\big(e^{\mathrm{i}z\delta}-1\big)\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-(\mathrm{i}z+\lambda)x}W^{\prime}(x)\,dx\bigg|^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big((z\delta)^{2}\wedge 1\big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}\Big)\,dz

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\int_{|z|\leq 1}(z\delta)^{2}\,dz+C\int_{1<|z|\leq 1/\delta}\delta^{2}\,dz+C\int_{|z|>1/\delta}\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}\,dz,

which can be uniformly bounded by
C\cdot\delta
.

\Box

Corollary 2.3

For each
T\geq 0
, there exist two constants
C>0
and
n_{0}\geq 1
such that for any
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T}\big|W^{\prime}(x+\delta)-W^{\prime}(x)\big|^{2}\,dx\leq C\cdot\delta.

Proof.
For any
\beta>0
, by the fact that
W^{\prime}(x)=0
for all
x<0
we have

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{T}\big|W^{\prime}(x+\delta)-W^{\prime}(x)\big|^{2}\,dx\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{T}\big|W^{\prime}(x+\delta)-W^{\prime}(x)\big|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle e^{2\beta(T+1)}\int_{-1}^{T}e^{-2\beta(x+\delta)}\big|W^{\prime}(x+\delta)-W^{\prime}(x)\big|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle e^{2\beta(T+1)}\int_{-1}^{T}\big|e^{-\beta(x+\delta)}W^{\prime}(x+\delta)-e^{-\beta x}W^{\prime}(x)\big|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle+e^{2\beta(T+1)}\int_{0}^{T}\big|\big(e^{-\beta\delta}-1\big)e^{-\beta x}W^{\prime}(x)\big|^{2}\,dx.

By Lemma
2.2
, the first term on the right side can be bounded by
C\cdot\delta
uniformly in
\delta\in(0,1)
.
By (
1.7
) and the inequality
|e^{-z}-1|\leq z
for any
z\geq 0
, the second term can be bounded by

\displaystyle e^{2\beta(T+1)}\int_{0}^{T}\beta^{2}e^{-2\beta x}\,dx\cdot\frac{\delta^{2}}{c^{2}}\leq C\cdot\delta^{2},

uniformly in
\delta\in(0,1)
and hence the desired upper bound holds.

\Box

Recall the function
\varPhi_{0}
defined in (
1.17
).
Let us consider the function

\displaystyle\varPhi_{\beta}(\lambda):=\varPhi_{0}(\lambda)+\beta\cdot\lambda=\varPhi(\lambda)+(\beta-b)\cdot\lambda,\quad\lambda\geq 0,

which is the Laplace exponent of a spectrally positive Lévy process with triplet
(\beta,c,\nu)
.
Denote by
W_{\beta}
and
W^{\prime}_{\beta}
the scale function associated to
\varPhi_{\beta}
and its right-derivative respectively.

Lemma 2.4

For all
x\geq 0
, we have

W^{\prime}(x)=W^{\prime}_{\beta}(x)+(\beta-b)\cdot W^{\prime}_{\beta}*W^{\prime}(x)
.

Proof.
It is obvious
W^{\prime}=W^{\prime}_{\beta}
if
\beta=b
. When
\beta\neq b
, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\varPhi_{\beta}(\lambda),\varPhi(\lambda)>0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{\varPhi_{\beta}(\lambda)}+(\beta-b)\cdot\frac{\lambda}{\varPhi_{\beta}(\lambda)}\cdot\frac{\lambda}{\varPhi(\lambda)}=\frac{\lambda\cdot\big(\varPhi(\lambda)+(\beta-b)\cdot\lambda\big)}{\varPhi_{\beta}(\lambda)\varPhi(\lambda)}=\frac{\lambda}{\varPhi(\lambda)},

which along with (
2.3
) and the continuity of
W^{\prime}_{\beta}
,
W^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
yields the desired identity.

\Box

2.2
Stochastic integrals driven by martingale measures

Let
B(ds,dz)
be a
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-Gaussian white noise on
(0,\infty)^{2}
with intensity
ds\,dz
and
\widetilde{N}(ds,dy,dz)
be a compensated
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-Poisson random measure on
(0,\infty)^{3}
with intensity
ds\,\mu(dy)\,dz
and
\mu(dy)
being a
\sigma
-finite measure on
(0,\infty)
.
For a
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-predictable non-negative process
X
, two measurable functions
f
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
g
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
satisfying that for any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}|f(s)|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}|g(s,y)|^{2}ds\,\mu(dy)<\infty,

we consider the following two stochastic Volterra integrals

\displaystyle\begin{split}B(f,t)&:=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X(s)}f(t-s)B(ds,dz),\cr\widetilde{N}(g,t)&:=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}g(t-s,y)\widetilde{N}(ds,dz,dy),\end{split}\qquad t\geq 0.

Proposition 2.5

For two constants
p\geq 1
and
T>0
, assume that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X(t)\big|^{p}\Big]<\infty\quad\mbox{and}\quad\int_{0}^{T}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|g(s,y)\big|^{2p}\mu(dy)<\infty.

Then there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
p
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|B(f,t)\big|^{2p}\Big]\leq C\cdot\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X(t)\big|^{p}\Big]\cdot\bigg|\int_{0}^{T}\big|f(s)\big|^{2}ds\bigg|^{p}

(2.5)

and

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|\widetilde{N}(g,t)\big|^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X(t)\big|^{p}\Big]\cdot\bigg|\int_{0}^{T}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|g(s,y)\big|^{2}\mu(dy)\bigg|^{p}

(2.7)

\displaystyle+C\cdot\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X(t)\big|\Big]\cdot\int_{0}^{T}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|g(s,y)\big|^{2p}\mu(dy).

Proof.
For each
t_{0}\in[0,T]
, we consider two auxiliary martingales
B_{t_{0}}(f,\cdot)
and
\widetilde{N}_{t_{0}}(g,\cdot)
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{t_{0}}(f,t)&:=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X(s)}f(t_{0}-s)B(ds,dz),\cr\widetilde{N}_{t_{0}}(g,t)&:=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}g(t_{0}-s,y)\widetilde{N}(ds,dz,dy),\end{split}\qquad t\geq 0.

It is obvious that
B(f,t_{0})\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}B_{t_{0}}(f,t_{0})
and
\widetilde{N}(g,t_{0})\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\widetilde{N}_{t_{0}}(g,t_{0})
.
Hence

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|B(f,t)\big|^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\sup_{t_{0}\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|B_{t_{0}}(f,t_{0})\big|^{2p}\Big],

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|\widetilde{N}(g,t)\big|^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\sup_{t_{0}\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|\widetilde{N}_{t_{0}}(g,t_{0})\big|^{2p}\Big].

By using the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality and then Hölder’s inequality as well as the change of variables, there exists a constant
C>0
that depends only on
p
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|B_{t_{0}}(f,t_{0})\big|^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}\big|B_{t_{0}}(f,t)\big|^{2p}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot\mathbf{E}\bigg[\Big|\int_{0}^{t_{0}}X(s)\cdot\big|f(t_{0}-s)\big|^{2}ds\Big|^{p}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\bigg[\Big|\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\big|f(t_{0}-s)\big|^{2}ds\Big|^{p-1}\cdot\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\big|X(s)\big|^{p}\cdot\big|f(t_{0}-s)\big|^{2}ds\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot\sup_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X(t)\big|^{p}\Big]\cdot\Big|\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\big|f(s)\big|^{2}ds\Big|^{p},

which immediately induces that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|B(f,t)\big|^{2p}\Big]=\sup_{t_{0}\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|B_{t_{0}}(f,t_{0})\big|^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X(t)\big|^{p}\Big]\cdot\Big|\int_{0}^{T}\big|f(s)\big|^{2}ds\Big|^{p}.

We now start to prove (
2.7
).
By using Theorem D.1 in
[
68
]
and then the change of variables to the following last inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|\widetilde{N}_{t_{0}}(g,t_{0})\big|^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}\big|\widetilde{N}_{t_{0}}(g,t)\big|^{2p}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot\sup_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X(t)\big|^{p}\Big]\cdot\bigg|\int_{0}^{t_{0}}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|g(t_{0}-s,y)\big|^{2}\mu(dy)\bigg|^{p}

\displaystyle+C\cdot\sup_{t\in[0,t_{0}]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X(t)\big|\Big]\cdot\int_{0}^{t_{0}}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|g(t_{0}-s,y)\big|^{2p}\mu(dy)

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X(t)\big|^{p}\Big]\cdot\bigg|\int_{0}^{T}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|g(s,y)\big|^{2}\mu(dy)\bigg|^{p}

\displaystyle+C\cdot\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X(t)\big|\Big]\cdot\int_{0}^{T}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|g(s,y)\big|^{2p}\mu(dy),

for some constant
C
depending only on
p
.
Hence the desired inequality (
2.7
) holds.

\Box

The next two stochastic Fubini’s theorems follow directly from Theorem 2.6 in
[
67
, p.296]
and Theorem D.2 in
[
68
]
respectively.

Proposition 2.6

Let
T\geq 0
,
m(dt)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
h
be a measurable function on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big|h(T-t)\big|\cdot\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}|f(s)|^{2}\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|g(s,y)\big|^{2}\,\mu(dy)\,ds\bigg)\,m(dt)<\infty.

If
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)<\infty
a.s., we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}h(T-t)B(f,t)\,m(dt)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{X(s)}\bigg(\int_{s}^{T}h(T-t)f(t-s)\,m(dt)\bigg)B(ds,dz)

(2.8)

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}h(T-t)\widetilde{N}(g,t)\,m(dt)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{X(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\int_{s}^{T}h(T-t)g(t-s,y)\,m(dt)\bigg)\widetilde{N}(ds,dz,dy).

(2.9)

3
Local times of compound Poisson processes

In this section, we first recall some properties of compound Poisson processes with unit negative drift and positive jumps and then two stochastic Volterra representations established in
[
68
]
for their local times.
Thereafter, we show that after a suitable scaling, their local times behave like those of
\xi
.

3.1
Compound Poisson processes

Let
\varPi(dy)
be a probability law on
(0,\infty)
with tail-distribution
\overline{\varPi}(y):=\varPi\big([y,\infty)\big)
and finite mean

\displaystyle\big\|\overline{\varPi}\big\|_{L^{1}}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\overline{\varPi}(y)\,dy=\int_{0}^{\infty}y\,\varPi(dy)<\infty.

Consider a compound Poisson process
Y:=\{Y(t):t\geq 0\}
defined on
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathscr{F}_{t},\mathbf{P})
with a drift
-1
, arrival rate
\gamma>0
and jump-size distribution
\varPi(dy)
.
It is a spectrally positive Lévy process with bounded variation and Laplace exponent
\varphi(\lambda):=\log\mathbf{E}[\exp\{-\lambda Y(1)\}]
being of the form

\displaystyle\varphi(\lambda)=\lambda+\gamma\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(e^{-\lambda y}-1\big)\,\varPi(dy)=\lambda\cdot\Big(1-\gamma\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda y}\,\overline{\varPi}(y)\,dy\Big),\quad\lambda\geq 0.

The function
\varphi
is infinitely differentiable and strictly convex on
(0,\infty)
with
\varphi(0)=0
and
\varphi(\infty)=\infty
.
In particular, it is strictly increasing on
[0,\infty)
if and only if

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(0)=-\mathbf{E}\big[Y(1)\big]=1-\gamma\cdot\big\|\overline{\varPi}\big\|_{L^{1}}\geq 0.

The process
Y
drifts to
-\infty
,
\infty
or is recurrent according as
\varphi^{\prime}(0)>0
,
<0
or
=0
.

Denote by
\tau^{+}_{Y}:=\inf\big\{t>0:Y(t)\geq 0\big\}
the first passage time of
Y
into
[0,\infty)
.
Actually, the process
Y
always gets into the positive half line by jumping, i.e.,
Y(\tau^{+}_{Y}-)<0
and
Y(\tau^{+}_{Y})>0
a.s.
By Theorem 17(ii) in
[
14
, p.204]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big(Y(\tau^{+}_{Y})\in dy\,\big|\,\tau^{+}_{Y}<\infty\big)=\varPi^{*}(dy),

where
\varPi^{*}(dy)
is the
size-biased distribution
of
\varPi(dy)
with probability density function

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(y):=\mathbf{1}_{\{y>0\}}\cdot\frac{\overline{\varPi}(y)}{\big\|\overline{\varPi}\big\|_{L^{1}}}.

Denote by
L_{Y}:=\{L_{Y}(x,t):x\in\mathbb{R},\,t\geq 0\}
the local times of
Y
satisfying the occupation density formula (
1.4
).
It also can be defined as the number of hitting times of
Y
at each level.
More precisely, the following identity holds

\displaystyle L_{Y}\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\big\{\#\{s\in(0,t]:Y(s-)=x\}:x\in\mathbb{R},\,t\geq 0\big\}

with
\#A
being the number of elements in the set
A
; see
[
14
, p.128]
.
The right-continuity of
Y
induces that the two-parameter process
L_{Y}
is jointly càdlàg almost surely; see
[
31
]
.
The total local time
L_{Y}(x,\infty)
at level
x
is almost surely infinite for some and hence all
x\in\mathbb{R}
if and only if
Y
is recurrent (
\varphi^{\prime}(0)=0
).
The right-inverse local time
\tau^{L}_{Y}:=\{\tau^{L}_{Y}(\zeta):\zeta\geq 0\}
at level
0
can be defined similarly as in (
1.5
).
For
a,\theta>0
, let
a\cdot Y(\theta\cdot):=\{aY(\theta t):t\geq 0\}
.
The identity (
1.4
) along with the change of variables and the right-continuity of local times implies the following two equivalences

\displaystyle L_{a\cdot Y(\theta\cdot)}\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\big\{(a\theta)^{-1}\cdot L_{Y}(x/a,\theta t):x\in\mathbb{R},t\geq 0\big\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tau^{L}_{a\cdot Y(\theta\cdot)}(\zeta)\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\theta^{-1}\cdot\tau^{L}_{Y}(a\theta\zeta).

(3.1)

It is obvious that
\tau^{L}_{Y}
only jumps at positive integer points.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we are interested in an
\mathbb{N}
-valued càdlàg process
L^{Y}_{k}:=\{L^{Y}_{k}(x):x\geq 0\}
defined as

L^{Y}_{k}
is the process
\{L_{Y}(x,\tau^{L}_{Y}(k)):x\geq 0\}
under
\mathbf{P}(\,\cdot\,|\tau^{L}_{Y}(k)<\infty)
.

3.2
Stochastic Volterra representation of
L^{Y}_{k}

Lambert
[
48
]
proved that the process
L^{Y}_{k}
equals in distribution to a homogeneous, binary Crump-Mode-Jagers branching process starting from
k
ancestors, in which the residual life of ancestors is distributed as
\varPi^{*}
, offsprings have a common life-length distribution
\varPi
and each individual gives birth to its children according to a Poisson process with rate
\gamma
.
Further, Xu
[
68
]
established the following Hawkes representations for the process
L^{Y}_{k}
by linking the branching process to a marked Hawkes process.

Lemma 3.1

Assume that
\varphi^{\prime}(0)\geq 0
, the process
L^{Y}_{k}
equals in distribution to the unique strong solution to the stochastic Volterra equation

\displaystyle Z_{k}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{\ell_{j}>t\}}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{Z_{k}(s-)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{y>t-s\}}\,N_{\varPi}(ds,dz,dy),\quad t\geq 0,

(3.2)

where
\{\ell_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
is a sequence of i.i.d
\mathscr{F}_{0}
-measurable random variables distributed as
\varPi^{*}(dy)
and
N_{\varPi}(ds,dz,dy)
is a
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-Poisson random measure on
(0,\infty)^{3}
with intensity
\gamma\cdot ds\,dz\,\varPi(dy)
.

Associated to the function
\gamma\cdot\overline{\varPi}
we define the
resolvent of the second kind

R_{\varPi}
by the unique global solution in
D(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R}_{+})
to the linear Volterra equation

\displaystyle R_{\varPi}(t)=\gamma\cdot\overline{\varPi}(t)+\gamma\cdot\overline{\varPi}*R_{\varPi}(t),\quad t\geq 0.

(3.3)

This equation is also known as
resolvent equation
or
renewal equation
.
Moreover, its unique solution admits the following Neumann series expansion

\displaystyle R_{\varPi}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\big(\gamma\cdot\overline{\varPi}\big)^{(*k)}(t),\quad t\geq 0,

(3.4)

where
f^{(*k)}
denotes the
k
-th convolution of
f
.
For convention, we extend
R_{\varPi}
to the whole line by setting
R_{\varPi}(t)=0
for
t<0
.
The function
R_{\varPi}
is integrable if and only if
\gamma\cdot\big\|\overline{\varPi}\big\|_{L^{1}}<1
; equivalently,
\varphi^{\prime}(0)>0
, in which case,

\displaystyle\big\|R_{\varPi}\big\|_{L^{1}}=\frac{\gamma\cdot\big\|\overline{\varPi}\big\|_{L^{1}}}{1-\gamma\cdot\big\|\overline{\varPi}\big\|_{L^{1}}}=\frac{\gamma\cdot\big\|\overline{\varPi}\big\|_{L^{1}}}{\varphi^{\prime}(0)}<\infty.

In addition, associated to
R_{\varPi}
we also introduce a two-parameter function
R
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by
R(t,y)=0
if
t<0
or
y<0
and

\displaystyle R(t,y)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{y>t\}}+\int_{0}^{t}R_{\varPi}(t-s)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{y>s\}}\,ds=\mathbf{1}_{\{y>t\}}+\int_{t-y}^{t}R_{\varPi}(s)\,ds,\quad t,\,y\geq 0.

(3.5)

We refer to
[
37
,
68
]
for more detailed and intuitive explanations of
R_{\varPi}
and
R
.
Armed with these two functions, we now recall the second stochastic Volterra equation established in
[
68
]
for
L^{Y}_{k}
.

Lemma 3.2

The stochastic Volterra equation (
3.2
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle Z_{k}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}R(t,\ell_{j})+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{Z_{k}(s-)}\int_{0}^{\infty}R(t-s,y)\,\widetilde{N}_{\varPi}(ds,dz,dy),\quad t\geq 0,

(3.6)

where
\widetilde{N}_{\varPi}(ds,dz,dy):=N_{\varPi}(ds,dz,dy)-\gamma\cdot ds\,dz\,\varPi(dy)
.

3.3
Poisson approximation

We now show that the local times of Lévy process
\xi
can be well-approximated by those of compound Poisson processes after a suitable scaling.
For
n\geq 1
, assume that the
n
-th compound Poisson process
Y_{n}:=\{Y_{n}(t):t\geq 0\}
has a drift
-1
, arrival rate
\gamma_{n}>0
and jump-size distribution
\varPi_{n}(dy)
on
(0,\infty)
.
Its Laplace exponent
\varphi_{n}
, local times
L_{Y_{n}}
and right-inverse local time
\tau^{L}_{Y_{n}}
at level
0
and the processes
L^{Y_{n}}_{k}
are defined as before.

Associated to a sequence of positive scaling parameters
\{\kappa_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
such that
\kappa_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, we define a sequence of rescaled processes
\{\xi^{(n)}\}_{n\geq 1}
by

\displaystyle\xi^{(n)}(t):=\frac{1}{n}\cdot Y_{n}(\kappa_{n}\cdot t),\quad t\geq 0,\,n\geq 1.

(3.7)

Denote by
\overline{\varPi}_{n}(y)
and
\varPi_{n}^{*}(dy)
the tail-distribution and size-biased distribution of
\varPi_{n}(dy)
respectively.
The process
\xi^{(n)}
is a spectrally positive Lévy process with Laplace exponent

\displaystyle\varPhi^{(n)}(\lambda):=\kappa_{n}\cdot\varphi_{n}\Big(\frac{\lambda}{n}\Big)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa_{n}}{n}\big(1-\gamma_{n}\cdot\big\|\overline{\varPi}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}\big)\cdot\lambda+\kappa_{n}\gamma_{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}y}-1+\frac{\lambda}{n}y\Big)\,\varPi_{n}(dy).\qquad

By Corollary 4.3 in
[
40
, p.440]
, the rescaled process

\xi^{(n)}
converges weakly to
\xi
in
D(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
as
n\to\infty
under the following necessary and sufficient condition.

Condition 3.3

The limit
\varPhi^{(n)}(\lambda)\to\varPhi(\lambda)
holds for all
\lambda\geq 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Define the local times
L_{\xi^{(n)}}
, the right-inverse local time
\tau^{L}_{\xi^{(n)}}
at level
0
and the process
L^{\xi^{(n)}}_{\zeta}
for
\zeta\geq 0
as before.
By the occupation density formula (
1.4
),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)L_{\xi^{(n)}}(x,t)dx\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\int_{0}^{t}f\big(\xi^{(n)}(r)\big)dr\overset{\rm d}{\to}\int_{0}^{t}f\big(\xi(r)\big)dr\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)L_{\xi}(x,t)dx,

as
n\to\infty
for all bounded and continuous function
f
on
\mathbb{R}
.
Hence the local times
L_{\xi}
can be obtained as the limit of
\{L_{\xi^{(n)}}\}_{n\geq 1}
.
This suggests us to approximate
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
by using
\big\{L^{\xi^{(n)}}_{\zeta}\big\}_{n\geq 1}
. Actually, it works in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions; see Theorem 2.4 in
[
49
]
.

Lemma 3.4

Under Condition
3.3
, we have
L^{\xi^{(n)}}_{\zeta}\overset{\rm f.d.d.}{\longrightarrow}L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
as
n\to\infty
.

At the end of this section, we construct another approximation for
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
by using solutions of the two equivalent stochastic Volterra equations (
3.2
) and (
3.6
).
For
n\geq 1
, let
R_{\varPi_{n}}
be the resolvent of the second kind associated to the function
\gamma_{n}\cdot\overline{\varPi}_{n}
and
R_{n}
the corresponding two-parameter function defined as in (
3.5
).
For
k\geq 1
, let
Z_{n,k}:=\{Z_{n,k}(t):t\geq 0\}
be the unique solution to

\displaystyle Z_{n,k}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}R_{n}(t,\ell_{n,j})+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{Z_{n,k}(s-)}\int_{0}^{\infty}R_{n}(t-s,y)\widetilde{N}_{\varPi_{n}}(ds,dz,dy)

(3.8)

with
\{\ell_{n,j}\}_{j\geq 1}
being a sequence of i.i.d
\mathscr{F}_{0}
-measurable random variables with common distribution
\varPi^{*}_{n}(dy)
and
\widetilde{N}_{\varPi_{n}}(ds,dz,dy)
being a
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-compensated Poisson random measure on
(0,\infty)^{3}
with intensity
\gamma_{n}\cdot ds\,dz\,\varPi_{n}(dy)
.
A direct consequence of Lemma
3.2
tells that
L^{Y_{n}}_{k}\overset{\rm d}{=}Z_{n,k}
.
For each
\zeta>0
, by (
3.7
) and (
3.1
) we have

\displaystyle L^{\xi^{(n)}}_{\zeta}\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\bigg\{\frac{n}{\kappa_{n}}\cdot L^{Y^{(n)}}_{[\zeta\cdot\kappa_{n}/n]}(nx):x\geq 0\bigg\}\overset{\rm d}{=}\bigg\{\frac{n}{\kappa_{n}}\cdot Z^{(n)}_{n,[\zeta\cdot\kappa_{n}/n]}(nt):t\geq 0\bigg\}=:X^{(n)}_{\zeta}.

(3.9)

Applying the change of variables to (
3.8
) induces that
X^{(n)}_{\zeta}
is the unique strong solution to

\displaystyle X_{\zeta}^{(n)}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{n}{\kappa_{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{[\zeta\cdot\kappa_{n}/n]}R_{n}(nt,\ell_{n,j})+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}^{(n)}(s-)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{n}{\kappa_{n}}\cdot R_{n}\big(n(t-s),y\big)\widetilde{N}^{(n)}(ds,dz,dy)\qquad

(3.10)

with
\widetilde{N}^{(n)}(ds,dz,dy):=\widetilde{N}_{\varPi_{n}}(n\cdot ds,n^{-1}\kappa_{n}\cdot dz,dy)
. The next corollary is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.4
.

Corollary 3.5

Under Condition
3.3
, we have
X^{(n)}_{\zeta}\overset{\rm f.d.d.}{\longrightarrow}L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
as
n\to\infty
.

4
Stochastic Volterra representation of
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}

In this section, we establish the stochastic Volterra representation for the process
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
by proving that the stochastic equation (
3.10
) converges weakly to (
1.9
).
As we have mentioned in the Introduction, the proof of tightness and the characterization of limit processes are usually quite challenging.
To overcome difficulties encountered in proving the weak convergence of (
3.10
), we need to specify the compound Poisson processes
\{Y_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
with easy-to-handle jump-size distributions and also establish limit theorems for stochastic Volterra integrals driven by martingale measures.

4.1
Asymptotic assumptions

Denote by
\boldsymbol{e}_{c}
,
\boldsymbol{E}_{c}
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{E}}_{c}
the exponential probability density function, distribution function and tail-distribution function with mean
c
respectively, i.e.,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{c}(y):=\frac{1}{c}\cdot e^{-y/c},\quad\boldsymbol{E}_{c}(y):=1-e^{-y/c}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\overline{\boldsymbol{E}}_{c}(y):=e^{-y/c},\quad y\geq 0.

Consider two non-negative sequences
\{\eta_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\{\theta_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset[0,1)
satisfying that

\bullet

\eta_{n}=0
and
\theta_{n}=c\cdot\bar{\nu}(0)\cdot n^{-2}
when
\bar{\nu}(0)<\infty
;

\bullet

n\theta_{n}\rightarrow 0
,
n^{2}\theta_{n}\to\infty
and
\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})=c^{-1}\cdot n^{2}\theta_{n}
when
\bar{\nu}(0)=\infty
.

For each
n\geq 1
, associated to
\eta_{n}
and
\nu(dy)
we define a probability law
\Lambda_{n}(dy)
on
(0,\infty)
by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}(dy):=\frac{1}{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})}\cdot\nu\Big(\eta_{n}+\frac{dy}{n}\Big)\quad\mbox{with}\quad\overline{\Lambda}_{n}(y):=\Lambda_{n}\big([y,\infty)\big)=\frac{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y/n)}{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})},\quad y\geq 0.

(4.1)

By the finite integral in (
1.2
), the law
\Lambda_{n}(dy)
has a finite mean

\displaystyle\big\|\overline{\Lambda}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}y\,\Lambda_{n}(dy)=\frac{n}{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)\,dy=\frac{c}{n\theta_{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)\,dy<\infty,

(4.2)

and its size-biased distribution
\Lambda_{n}^{*}(dy)
has density function

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}^{*}(y):=\frac{\overline{\Lambda}_{n}(y)}{\|\overline{\Lambda}_{n}\|_{L^{1}}}=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y/n)}{\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+z)\,dz},\quad y>0.

(4.3)

Proposition 4.1

As
n\to\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)}{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})}\,dy\to\begin{cases}\displaystyle\bar{\bar{\nu}}(0)/\bar{\nu}(0),&\mbox{if $\bar{\nu}(0)<\infty$};\\
0,&\mbox{if $\bar{\nu}(0)=\infty$},\end{cases}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)\,dy\to 0.

Proof.
The second limit follows directly from the first one and the fact that
n^{-1}\cdot\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})\to 0
.
For the first limit, it is obvious when
\bar{\nu}(0)<\infty
.
When
\bar{\nu}(0)=\infty
, the monotonicity of
\bar{\nu}
induces that for
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)}{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})}\,dy\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\frac{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)}{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})}\,dy+\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\frac{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)}{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})}\,dy\leq\epsilon+\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\frac{\bar{\nu}(y)}{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})}\,dy,

which goes to
0
by letting
n\to\infty
and then
\epsilon\to 0+
.

\Box

For each
n\geq 1
, assume that the
n
-th compound Poisson process
Y_{n}
has arrival rate

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}=\frac{1}{c}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{b}{n}-\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}y\,\nu(\eta_{n}+dy)\Big)^{+}=\frac{1}{c}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{b}{n}-\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)\,dy\Big)^{+}\leq\frac{1}{c},

(4.4)

which goes to
1/c
as
n\to\infty
,
and its jump-size distribution
\varPi_{n}(dy)
on
(0,\infty)
has density function

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(y)\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle(1-\theta_{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{c}(y)+\theta_{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{c}*d\Lambda_{n}(y),\quad y\geq 0.

(4.5)

Integrating both sides over
[x,\infty)
and then using Fubini’s theorem show that
\varPi_{n}(dy)
has tail-distribution

\displaystyle\overline{\varPi}_{n}(x):=\varPi_{n}\big([x,\infty)\big)=\overline{\boldsymbol{E}}_{c}(x)+\theta_{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{c}*\overline{\Lambda}_{n}(x),\quad x\geq 0.

(4.6)

In addition, by the change of variables and (
4.2
),

\displaystyle\big\|\overline{\varPi}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}=c+\theta_{n}\cdot\big\|\overline{\Lambda}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle c+\frac{n\theta_{n}}{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)\,dy

(4.7)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle c\cdot\Big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)\,dy\Big),

(4.8)

which along with (
4.4
) induces that

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}\cdot\big\|\overline{\varPi}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}=1-\frac{b}{n}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\cdot\Big(b+\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)\,dy\Big)\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+z)\,dz\in(0,1),

(4.9)

and further as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}\cdot\big\|\overline{\varPi}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}\to 1\quad\mbox{and}\quad n\cdot\big(1-\gamma_{n}\cdot\big\|\overline{\varPi}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}\big)\to b.

(4.10)

Recall the rescaled process
\xi^{(n)}
defined by (
3.7
) with
\kappa_{n}=n^{2}
.
It has Laplace exponent
\varPhi^{(n)}
as follows

\displaystyle\varPhi^{(n)}(\lambda)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle n\cdot\big(1-\gamma_{n}\cdot\big\|\overline{\varPi}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}\big)\cdot\lambda+n^{2}\gamma_{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}y}-1+\frac{\lambda}{n}y\Big)\pi_{n}(y)\,dy.

(4.11)

Proposition 4.2

The sequence
\big\{\varPhi^{(n)}\big\}_{n\geq 1}
satisfies Condition
3.3
.

Proof.
For each
\lambda\geq 0
, plugging (
4.5
) into (
4.11
) and then using (
4.10
) induce that as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\varPhi^{(n)}(\lambda)\!\!\!

\displaystyle\sim\!\!\!

\displaystyle b\cdot\lambda+\frac{n^{2}}{c}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}y}-1+\frac{\lambda}{n}y\Big)\boldsymbol{e}_{c}(y)\,dy

(4.13)

\displaystyle+\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}y}-1+\frac{\lambda}{n}y\Big)\boldsymbol{e}_{c}*d\Lambda_{n}(y)\,dy.

Applying the dominated convergence theorem and the fact that
e^{-z}-1+z\sim z^{2}/2
as
z\to 0
to the second term on the right side of (
4.13
),

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{n^{2}}{c}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}y}-1+\frac{\lambda}{n}y\Big)\boldsymbol{e}_{c}(y)\,dy=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2c}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{2}\boldsymbol{e}_{c}(y)\,dy=c\cdot\lambda^{2}.

An application of the basic property of convolution to the third term shows that it equals to

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})\cdot\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}y}\boldsymbol{e}_{c}(y)\,dy\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}z}\Lambda_{n}(dz)-1+c\cdot\frac{\lambda}{n}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda}{n}y\,\Lambda_{n}(dy)\bigg),

which can be written as the sum of the following three terms

\displaystyle A_{1}^{(n)}(\lambda)\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})}{n}\cdot c\cdot\lambda,

\displaystyle A_{2}^{(n)}(\lambda)\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}y}-1+\frac{\lambda}{n}y\Big)\,\Lambda_{n}(dy),

\displaystyle A_{3}^{(n)}(\lambda)\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}y}-1\Big)\boldsymbol{e}_{c}(y)\,dy\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}z}\Lambda_{n}(dz).

The limit
n^{-1}\cdot\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})\to 0
immediately yields that
|A_{1}^{(n)}(\lambda)|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
Also, the inequality
|e^{-z}-1|\leq z
for any
z\geq 0
induces that

\displaystyle\big|A_{3}^{(n)}(\lambda)\big|\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}y\,\boldsymbol{e}_{c}(y)\,dy\cdot\frac{\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n})}{n}\to 0.

By using (
4.1
), the change of variables and then the vague convergence of
\nu(\eta_{n}+dy)
to
\nu(dy)
,

\displaystyle A_{2}^{(n)}(\lambda)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(e^{-\lambda\frac{y}{n}}-1+\lambda\frac{y}{n}\Big)\,\nu\Big(\eta_{n}+\frac{dy}{n}\Big)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(e^{-\lambda y}-1+\lambda y\big)\,\nu(\eta_{n}+dy)

\displaystyle\to\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(e^{-\lambda y}-1+\lambda y\big)\,\nu(dy),

as
n\to\infty
.
Taking all preceding limits back into (
4.13
)
induces the limit
\varPhi^{(n)}(\lambda)\to\varPhi(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\geq 0
.

\Box

By Proposition
4.2
and Corollary
3.5
, the process
L^{\xi}_{\zeta}
can be well-approximated in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions by the solution
X^{(n)}_{\zeta}
of (
3.10
) with
\kappa_{n}=n^{2}
.
Before establishing the weak convergence of
\{X^{(n)}_{\zeta}\}_{n\geq 1}
, we first provide a stochastic Volterra equation that is equivalent to (
3.10
) and much easy to be dealt with.
By (
4.6
) and (
4.7
), the size-biased distribution
\varPi^{*}_{n}(dy)
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
of
\varPi_{n}(dy)
has probability density function

\displaystyle\pi^{*}_{n}(y):=\frac{\overline{\varPi}_{n}(y)}{\big\|\overline{\varPi}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}}\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\boldsymbol{E}}_{c}(y)}{\big\|\overline{\varPi}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}}+\frac{\theta_{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{c}*\overline{\Lambda}_{n}(y)}{\big\|\overline{\varPi}_{n}\big\|_{L^{1}}}=p_{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{c}(y)+(1-p_{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{c}*\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}_{n}(y),

(4.14)

which is a weighted sum of the two probability density functions
\boldsymbol{e}_{c}
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{c}*\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}_{n}
with weight

\displaystyle p_{n}:=\Big(1+\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)\,dy\Big)^{-1}=1-\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(\eta_{n}+y)\,dy+o\big(1/n\big),

(4.15)

as
n\to\infty
.
Let
B_{n}^{c}(\zeta)
be a binomial random variable with number of trials
[n\zeta]
and success probability
p_{n}
.
Moreover, let
B_{n}^{\Lambda}(\zeta):=[n\zeta]-B^{c}_{n}(\zeta)
, which is also binomially distributed with parameters
([n\zeta],1-p_{n})
.
Consider two independent sequences of i.i.d. non-negative random variables
\{\ell_{n,j}^{c}\}_{j\geq 1}
and
\{\ell_{n,j}^{\Lambda}\}_{j\geq 1}
satisfying that

\bullet

\ell_{n,j}^{c}
has probability density function
\boldsymbol{e}_{c}
;

\bullet

\ell_{n,j}^{\Lambda}
has probability density function
\boldsymbol{e}_{c}*\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{*}_{n}
.

For each
j\geq 1
, the random variable
\ell_{n,j}
can be realized by setting
\ell_{n,j}=\ell_{n,j}^{c}
with probability
p_{n}
and
\ell_{n,j}=\ell_{n,j}^{\Lambda}
with probability
1-p_{n}
.
Consequently, one can identify that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{[n\zeta]}R_{n}(\cdot,\ell_{n,j})\overset{\rm d}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{B_{n}^{c}(\zeta)}R_{n}(\cdot,\ell_{n,i}^{c})+\sum_{j=1}^{B_{n}^{\Lambda}(\zeta)}R_{n}(\cdot,\ell_{n,j}^{\Lambda}).

Note that the compensated Poisson random measure
\widetilde{N}^{(n)}(ds,dz,dy)=\widetilde{N}_{\varPi_{n}}(n\cdot ds,n\cdot dz,dy)
on
(0,\infty)^{3}
has intensity
n^{2}\gamma_{n}\cdot ds\,dz\,\varPi_{n}(dy)
, which, by (
4.5
), can be written as

\displaystyle n^{2}\gamma_{n}(1-\theta_{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{c}(y)\,\cdot ds\,dz\,dy+n^{2}\gamma_{n}\theta_{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{c}*d\Lambda_{n}(y)\,\cdot ds\,dz\,dy.

(4.16)

This allows us to define on an extension of the original probability space two orthogonal compensated Poisson random measures
\widetilde{N}^{(n)}_{c}(ds,dz,dy)
and
\widetilde{N}^{(n)}_{\Lambda}(ds,dz,dy)
on
(0,\infty)^{3}
with intensities being the two terms in (
4.16
) respectively such that

\displaystyle\widetilde{N}^{(n)}(ds,dz,dy)=\widetilde{N}^{(n)}_{c}(ds,dz,dy)+\widetilde{N}^{(n)}_{\Lambda}(ds,dz,dy).

In conclusion, the preceding arguments and notation allow us to write the stochastic Volterra equation (
3.10
) into the following equivalent form

\displaystyle X_{\zeta}^{(n)}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathbf{I}^{(n)}_{i}(t),\quad t\geq 0,

(4.17)

in which the four summands on the right side are given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}^{(n)}_{1}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{B_{n}^{c}(\zeta)}R_{n}(nt,\ell_{n,j}^{c}),\quad\ \ \,\mathbf{I}^{(n)}_{2}(t):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{B_{n}^{\Lambda}(\zeta)}R_{n}(nt,\ell_{n,j}^{\Lambda}),

(4.18)

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}^{(n)}_{3}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}^{(n)}(s-)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\cdot R_{n}\big(n(t-s),y\big)\widetilde{N}^{(n)}_{c}(ds,dz,dy),

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}^{(n)}_{4}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}^{(n)}(s-)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\cdot R_{n}\big(n(t-s),y\big)\widetilde{N}^{(n)}_{\Lambda}(ds,dz,dy).

(4.20)

In the following subsections, we will prove the jointly weak convergence of the five terms in (
4.17
) to the corresponding terms in (
1.9
).

4.2
Auxiliary lemmas

4.2.1
Asymptotics of rescaled resolvent

In view of (
4.18
)-(
4.20
), the two time-scaled functions
R_{\varPi_{n}}(n\cdot)
and
R_{n}(n\cdot,y)
play an important role in the convergence of
\{X^{(n)}_{\zeta}\}_{n\geq 1}
.
By (
3.3
) and (
3.5
), we have for
t\geq 0
and
y>0
,

\displaystyle R_{\varPi_{n}}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}\cdot\overline{\varPi}_{n}(t)+\gamma_{n}\overline{\varPi}_{n}*R_{\varPi_{n}}(t),

(4.21)

\displaystyle R_{n}(t,y)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle 1_{\{y>t\}}+\int_{t-y}^{t}R_{\varPi_{n}}(s)\,ds.

(4.22)

In this section, we mainly study their asymptotic properties including uniform upper bounds and
L^{p}
-convergence with the help of the following upper-bound estimates for probability density functions of geometric random sums.

Proposition 4.3

Consider a geometrically distributed random variable
N_{q}
with parameter
q\in(0,1)
and two independent sequences of i.i.d. non-negative random variables
\{e_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and
\{U_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
.
If
e_{i}
is exponentially distributed with mean
\lambda>0
, then the geometric sum
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{q}}(e_{i}+U_{i})
has a density function uniformly bounded by
q/\lambda
.

Proof.
Let
F_{U}(du)
be the probability law of
U_{1}
and
e_{k,\lambda}
the density function of
\sum_{i=1}^{k}e_{i}
for
k\geq 1
.
The density function of geometric sum
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{q}}(e_{i}+U_{i})
admits the following representation

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}q(1-q)^{k-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{k}}e_{k,\lambda}(x-u_{1}-\cdots-u_{k})\prod_{i=1}^{k}F_{U}(du_{i})

\displaystyle=\!\!\!
