0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq t
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,5}(t_{2})-\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,5}(t_{1})\big|^{2p}\Big]\leq C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot\big(1+W(t)\big)^{2p-2}\cdot(1+t)^{2p}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{p}.

Proof.
Similarly as in the proof of Proposition
7.4
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,5}(t_{2})-\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,5}(t_{1})=\int_{0}^{t_{2}}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{t_{2}-s-y}^{t_{2}-s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr-\int_{t_{1}-s-y}^{t_{1}-s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr\Big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)

and there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
p
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,5}(t_{2})-\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,5}(t_{1})\big|^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot\sup_{s\in[0,t_{2}]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X_{\zeta}(s)\big|^{p}\Big]\cdot\big|A_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})\big|^{p}

(7.8)

\displaystyle+C\cdot\sup_{s\in[0,t_{2}]}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|X_{\zeta}(s)\big|\Big]\cdot A_{2p}(t_{1},t_{2}),

where

\displaystyle A_{2k}(t_{1},t_{2})\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{2}}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{t_{2}-s-y}^{t_{2}-s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr-\int_{t_{1}-s-y}^{t_{1}-s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr\Big)^{2k}\,\nu(dy),\quad k\geq 1.

For
k=1
, by the fact that
W^{\prime}_{\beta}(x)=0
if
x<0
and the change of variables,

\displaystyle A_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{t_{2}-s-y}^{t_{2}-s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr\Big)^{2}\,\nu(dy)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t_{1}}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{t_{2}-s-y}^{t_{2}-s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr-\int_{t_{1}-s-y}^{t_{1}-s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr\Big)^{2}\,\nu(dy)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{2}-t_{1}}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{s-y}^{s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr\Big)^{2}\,\nu(dy)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t_{1}}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{t_{2}-t_{1}+s-y}^{t_{2}-t_{1}+s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr-\int_{s-y}^{s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr\Big)^{2}\,\nu(dy)

By (
6.8
) and the inequality
\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}W_{\beta}(t)<\infty
, we have
\int_{s-y}^{s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr\leq C(1\wedge y)
uniformly in
s,y\geq 0
, which along with (
1.2
) induces that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{2}-t_{1}}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{s-y}^{s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr\Big)^{2}\,\nu(dy)\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\int_{0}^{t_{2}-t_{1}}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}(1\wedge y^{2})\,\nu(dy)\leq C\cdot\big|t_{2}-t_{1}\big|,

uniformly in
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<t
.
Additionally, by using the change of variables and then Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{1}}ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{t_{2}-t_{1}+s-y}^{t_{2}-t_{1}+s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr-\int_{s-y}^{s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr\Big)^{2}\,\nu(dy)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{s-y}^{s}\big(W^{\prime}_{\beta}(t_{2}-t_{1}+r)-W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)\big)dr\Big)^{2}\,ds\,\nu(dy)

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t_{1}}(s\wedge y)\int_{s-y}^{s}\big(W^{\prime}_{\beta}(t_{2}-t_{1}+r)-W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)\big)^{2}dr\,ds,

which can be divided into the following two terms

\displaystyle B_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t_{1}}(s\wedge y)\int_{(s-y)\wedge 0}^{0}\big(W^{\prime}_{\beta}(t_{2}-t_{1}+r)\big)^{2}dr\,ds,

\displaystyle B_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t_{1}}(s\wedge y)\int_{(s-y)\vee 0}^{s}\big(W^{\prime}_{\beta}(t_{2}-t_{1}+r)-W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)\big)^{2}dr\,ds.

By (
6.8
) as well as the two facts that
W^{\prime}_{\beta}(x)=0
if
x<0
and
W_{\beta}(\infty)<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{(s-y)\wedge 0}^{0}\big(W^{\prime}_{\beta}(t_{2}-t_{1}+r)\big)^{2}dr\,ds\leq C\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{y>s\}},

uniformly in
x,y\geq 0
and hence

\displaystyle B_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t_{1}}(s\wedge y)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{y>s\}}\,ds=C\int_{0}^{t_{1}}s\bar{\nu}(s)\,ds\leq C\cdot(1+t)^{2},

uniformly in
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<t
.
For
B_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})
, by Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle B_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\big(W^{\prime}_{\beta}(t_{2}-t_{1}+s)-W^{\prime}_{\beta}(s)\big)^{2}\int_{s}^{(s+y)\wedge t_{1}}(r\wedge y)dr\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(t_{1}\wedge y)^{2}\nu(dy)\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(W^{\prime}_{\beta}(t_{2}-t_{1}+s)-W^{\prime}_{\beta}(s)\big)^{2}ds,

which can be bounded by
C\cdot(1+t)^{2}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|
uniformly in
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<t
; see (
7.6
).
Putting all preceding estimates together induces that

\displaystyle A_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})\leq C\cdot(1+t)^{2}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|.

(7.9)

for some constant
C
independent of
t,t_{1},t_{2}
.
Similarly as in (
4.69
)-(
4.72
) we also have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{t_{2}-s-y}^{t_{2}-s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr-\int_{t_{1}-s-y}^{t_{1}-s}W^{\prime}_{\beta}(r)dr\Big|\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{2}{c}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|

and then
A_{2p}(t,\delta)\leq C\cdot A_{2}(t,\delta)\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{2p-2}\leq C\cdot(1+t)^{2}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{2p-1}.

Taking this and (
7.9
) back into (
7.8
) and then using (
1.25
), there exists constant
C>0
such that for any
t\geq 0
and
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq t
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,5}(t_{2})-\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,5}(t_{1})\big|^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot(1+W(t))^{2p-2}(1+t)^{2p}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{p}

\displaystyle+C\cdot\zeta\cdot(1+t)^{2}\cdot|t_{2}-t_{1}|\cdot(|t_{2}-t_{1}|\wedge W(t_{2}))^{2p-2},

and hence the desired upper bound holds.

\Box

Armed with the preceding propositions, we are now ready to prove Theorem
1.9
.
To obtain the desired uniform upper bound for all moments of the Hölder coefficient, we need the Garsia-Rodemich-Rumsey inequality; see e.g. Lemma 1.1 in
[
34
]
with
\psi(u)=|u|^{2p}
and
p(u)=|u|^{q+\frac{1}{2p}}
for
p,q>0
such that
2pq>1
or Theorem 1.1 in
[
67
]
.
It states that for a continuous function
f
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, there exists a constant
C_{p,q}>0
depending only on
p
and
q
such that for any
x_{2}>x_{1}\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\big|f(x_{2})-f(x_{1})\big|^{2p}\leq C_{p,q}\cdot\big|x_{2}-x_{1}\big|^{2pq-1}\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}ds\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}\frac{|f(s)-f(r)|^{2p}}{|s-r|^{2pq+1}}dr.

(7.10)

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

The Kolmogorov continuity theorem along with Proposition
7.1
-
7.5
yields that
\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,1}
is Lipschitz continuous and other four terms in (
7.3
),
\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,i}
with
i=2,\cdots,5
, are all Hölder continuous with exponent in
(0,1/2)
.
Consequently, the solution
X_{\zeta}
is Hölder continuous with exponent strictly less than
1/2
.

We now prove the first upper bound in (
1.26
).
For
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
, by the power inequality, there exists constant
C>0
depending only on
p
such that for any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\big\|X_{\zeta}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{5}\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,i}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbf{E}\Big[\big\|X_{\zeta}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\Big]\leq C\cdot\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,1}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}+C\sum_{i=2}^{5}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,i}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\Big].

(7.11)

By Lemma
7.1
with
\beta>0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,1}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta^{2p}\cdot\beta^{2p}\cdot\sup_{0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq t}\frac{|W_{\beta}(t_{2})-W_{\beta}(t_{1})|^{2p}}{|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{2p\kappa}}\leq\zeta^{2p}\cdot\frac{\beta^{2p}}{c^{2p\kappa}}\cdot\big|W_{\beta}(t)\big|^{2p(1-\kappa)}\leq C\cdot\zeta^{2p}.

We now provide the upper bound estimates for the last four expectations in (
7.11
) by using the inequality (
7.10
).
More precisely, an applications of the inequality (
7.10
) to
\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}
shows that

\displaystyle\big|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}(t_{2})-\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}(t_{1})\big|^{2p}\leq C_{p,q}\cdot\big|t_{2}-t_{1}\big|^{2pq-1}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}ds\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\frac{|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}(s)-\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}(r)|^{2p}}{|s-r|^{2pq+1}}dr

and then

\displaystyle\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C_{p,q}\cdot\sup_{0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq t}\big|t_{2}-t_{1}\big|^{2p(q-\kappa)-1}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}ds\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\frac{|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}(s)-\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}(r)|^{2p}}{|s-r|^{2pq+1}}dr.

In particular, by choosing
p>(1-2\kappa)^{-1}
and
q=\frac{1}{2p}+\kappa
we have

\displaystyle\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C_{p,q}\int_{0}^{t}ds\int_{0}^{t}\frac{|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}(s)-\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}(r)|^{2p}}{|s-r|^{2p\kappa+2}}dr.

Taking expectations on both sides and then using Fubini’s theorem as well as Proposition
7.2
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C_{p,q}\int_{0}^{t}ds\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathbf{E}\big[|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}(s)-\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,2}(r)|^{2p}\big]}{|s-r|^{2p\kappa+2}}dr

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot\int_{0}^{t}ds\int_{0}^{t}|s-r|^{p-2p\kappa-2}dr\leq C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot t^{p-2p\kappa},

uniformly in
\zeta,t\geq 0
.
Similarly, with the help of Lemma
7.3
-
7.5
, we can also prove that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,3}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{2p})\cdot\big(1+W(t)\big)^{4p-2}\cdot t^{2p(1-\kappa)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,4}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot\big(1+W(t)\big)^{2p-2}\cdot t^{p-2p\kappa},

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big\|\widetilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\beta,5}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\Big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{p})\cdot\big(1+W(t)\big)^{2p-2}\cdot(1+t)^{2p}\cdot t^{p-2p\kappa}.

Plugging all preceding upper bound estimates into the right side of the second inequality in (
7.11
) and then using the inequality
W(t)\leq t/c
for all
t\geq 0
, we have uniformly in
\zeta,t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big\|X_{\zeta}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\Big]\leq C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{2p})\cdot(1+t)^{2p(3-\kappa)}.

For the second upper bound in (
1.26
),
by the triangle inequality and Hölder continuity we have

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big|X_{\zeta}(s)\big|\leq\zeta+\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big|X_{\zeta}(s)-\zeta\big|\leq\zeta+\big\|X_{\zeta}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}\cdot t^{\kappa},

for any
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
.
Then by the power inequality and the preceding results,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\bigg[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big|X_{\zeta}(s)\big|^{2p}\bigg]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot\zeta^{2p}+C\cdot\mathbf{E}\Big[\big\|X_{\zeta}\big\|_{C^{0,\kappa}_{t}}^{2p}\Big]\cdot t^{2p\kappa}\leq C\cdot(\zeta\vee\zeta^{2p})\cdot(1+t)^{6p},

for some constant
C>0
independent of
\zeta
and
t
.

\Box

8
Nonlinear Volterra equation and Laplace functionals

This section is devoted to provide detailed proof for Theorem
1.10
as well as the proof of weak uniqueness of solution to (
1.9
).
It consists of two subsections in which the well-posedness of the nonlinear Volterra equation (
1.27
) and the affine representation for the Laplace functional of solutions to (
1.9
) are established respectively.

8.1
Well-posedness of nonlinear Volterra equation

As a preparation, we first provide in the next two propositions an upper bounded estimate and Lipschitz continuity of the nonlinear operator
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}
.
For convention, we write
\mathcal{V}\circ f(t)
for the second term on the right side of (
1.28
) and hence

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ f(t)=c\cdot\big(f(t)\big)^{2}+\mathcal{V}\circ f(t).

(8.1)

Proposition 8.1

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
f\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
and
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle 0\leq\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ f\big)*W^{\prime}(t)\leq C\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\cdot(1+t)^{2}\cdot\big\|f\big\|_{L^{2}_{t}}^{2}.

Proof.
By (
8.1
), we have
\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ f\big)*W^{\prime}(t)=c\cdot|f|^{2}*W^{\prime}(t)+(\mathcal{V}\circ f)*W^{\prime}(t)
. By (
1.7
),

\displaystyle 0\leq c\cdot|f|^{2}*W^{\prime}(t)=c\int_{0}^{t}|f(s)|^{2}W^{\prime}(t-s)ds\leq\int_{0}^{t}|f(s)|^{2}ds.

(8.2)

Moreover, applying the inequality
0\leq e^{-z}-1+z\leq|z|^{2}\cdot e^{|z|}
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
to
\mathcal{V}\circ f(t)
induces that

\displaystyle 0\leq\mathcal{V}\circ f(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\Big\{\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}\big|f(r)\big|\,dr\Big\}\Big(\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}f(r)\,dr\Big)^{2}\nu(dy)

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}f(r)\,dr\Big)^{2}\nu(dy).

By using Hölder’s inequality to the last inner integral, we further have

\displaystyle 0\leq\mathcal{V}\circ f(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{0}^{\infty}(t\wedge y)\nu(dy)\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}|f(r)|^{2}\,dr,

which along with (
1.7
) and Fubini’s theorem yields that

\displaystyle 0\leq\big(\mathcal{V}\circ f\big)*W^{\prime}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\int_{0}^{t}\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}\cdot s\big\}\int_{0}^{\infty}(s\wedge y)\nu(dy)\int_{(s-y)^{+}}^{s}|f(r)|^{2}\,dr\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{0}^{\infty}(t\wedge y)\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(s-y)^{+}}^{s}|f(r)|^{2}\,dr\,ds

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{0}^{t}y\,\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(s-y)^{+}}^{s}|f(r)|^{2}\,dr\,ds

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{t}^{\infty}t\,\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{(s-y)^{+}}^{s}|f(r)|^{2}\,dr\,ds.

The first term on the right side of the last equality can be bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{0}^{t}y\,\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t+y}\int_{(s-y)^{+}}^{s\wedge t}|f(r)|^{2}\,dr\,ds

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{0}^{t}y\,\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t}|f(s)|^{2}\int_{s}^{s+y}\,dr\,ds

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{0}^{t}y^{2}\,\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t}|f(s)|^{2}\,ds.

Here the first equality is obtained by using Fubini’s theorem.
Moreover, the second term equals to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{t}^{\infty}t\,\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}|f(r)|^{2}\,dr\,ds\leq\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{t}^{\infty}t^{2}\,\nu(dy)\int_{0}^{t}|f(s)|^{2}\,ds.

Putting these results together and then using (
1.2
), we have

\displaystyle 0\leq\big(\mathcal{V}\circ f\big)*W^{\prime}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\int_{0}^{\infty}(t\wedge y)^{2}\,\nu(dy)\cdot\int_{0}^{t}|f(s)|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle C\cdot\exp\big\{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\cdot t\big\}\cdot(1+t)^{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}|f(s)|^{2}\,ds,

for some constant
C>0
independent of
f
and
t
.
The desired upper bound follows by combining this together with (
8.2
).

\Box

Proposition 8.2

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
and
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{2}\big)*W^{\prime}(t)\Big|\leq C\cdot\exp\big\{(\|f_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}})\cdot(1+t)\big\}\cdot(1+t)^{2}\cdot\big\|f_{1}-f_{2}\big\|_{L^{1}_{t}}.

(8.3)

Proof.
By (
8.1
), we first have

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ f_{2}\big)*W^{\prime}(t)\Big|\leq c\cdot\Big||f_{1}|^{2}*W^{\prime}(t)-|f_{2}|^{2}*W^{\prime}(t)\Big|+\Big|\big(\mathcal{V}\circ f_{1}-\mathcal{V}\circ f_{1}\big)*W^{\prime}(t)\Big|.

By using (
1.7
) to the first term on the right side,

\displaystyle c\cdot\Big||f_{1}|^{2}*W^{\prime}(t)-|f_{2}|^{2}*W^{\prime}(t)\Big|\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\big||f_{1}(s)|^{2}-|f_{2}(s)|^{2}\big|\,ds

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}|f_{1}(s)+f_{2}(s)|\cdot|f_{1}(s)-f_{2}(s)|\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\big(\|f_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\big)\cdot\int_{0}^{t}|f_{1}(s)-f_{2}(s)|\,ds.

Additionally, an application of the next inequality that can be proved immediately by using the mean-value theorem

\displaystyle\big|(e^{-x}-1+x)-(e^{-z}-1+z)\big|\leq e^{|x|+|z|}\cdot\big(|x|+|z|\big)\cdot|x-z|,\quad x,z\in\mathbb{R},

to
\big|\mathcal{V}\circ f_{1}(t)-\mathcal{V}\circ f_{2}(t)\big|
induces that it can be bounded by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}\big(|f_{1}(r)|+|f_{2}(r)|\big)\,dr}\cdot\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}\big(|f_{1}(r)|+|f_{2}(r)|\big)\,dr\cdot\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}\big|f_{1}(r)-f_{2}(r)\big|\,dr\,\nu(dy)

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\exp\big\{(\|f_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}})\cdot t\big\}\cdot\big(\|f_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}\big)\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}(t\wedge y)\int_{(t-y)^{+}}^{t}\big|f_{1}(r)-f_{2}(r)\big|\,dr\,\nu(dy).

This together with (
1.7
) induces that

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\mathcal{V}\circ f_{1}-\mathcal{V}\circ f_{1}\big)*W^{\prime}(t)\Big|\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\int_{0}^{t}\big|\mathcal{V}\circ f_{1}(s)-\mathcal{V}\circ f_{2}(s)\big|\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\cdot\exp\big\{(\|f_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}})\cdot(1+t)\big\}

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{t}\,ds\int_{0}^{\infty}(s\wedge y)\int_{(s-y)^{+}}^{s}\big|f_{1}(r)-f_{2}(r)\big|\,dr\,\nu(dy).

Similarly as in the proof of Proposition
8.1
, there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
t
and
f_{1},f_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\,ds\int_{0}^{\infty}(s\wedge y)\int_{(s-y)^{+}}^{s}\big|f_{1}(r)-f_{2}(r)\big|\,dr\,\nu(dy)\leq C\cdot(1+t)^{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\big|f_{1}(s)-f_{2}(s)\big|\,ds.

The desired upper bound (
8.3
) follows directly by putting all preceding result together.

\Box

A function
V_{\mu}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
is said to be a
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
-noncontinuable solution
of (
1.27
) if there exists a constant
T_{\infty}\in(0,\infty]
such that
V_{\mu}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
for any
T\in(0,T_{\infty})
and
\big\|V_{\mu}\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{\infty}}}=\infty
if
T_{\infty}<\infty
.
Moreover, it turns to be a
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
-global solution of (
1.27
) if
T_{\infty}=\infty
.
In the next lemma, we show that the existence of
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
-noncontinuable solution to (
1.27
) immediately induces that the global solution uniquely exists.

Lemma 8.3

If (
1.27
) has a
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
-noncontinuable solution, then it has a unique global solution
V_{\mu}\in D(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R}_{+})
that satisfies

\displaystyle V_{\mu}(t)\leq\frac{\mu([0,t])}{c},\quad t\geq 0.

Proof.
Assume that
(\widetilde{V}_{\mu},T_{\infty})
is a
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
-noncontinuable solution.
By Proposition
8.1
and (
1.7
), we have for almost every
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{\mu}(t)\leq W^{\prime}*d\mu(t)\leq\frac{\mu([0,t])}{c}.

For any
T\in[0,T_{\infty})
, by using Proposition
8.1
again there exists a constant
C_{T}>0
such that

\displaystyle-\widetilde{V}_{\mu}(t)=-W^{\prime}*d\mu(t)+\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ\widetilde{V}_{\mu}\big)*W^{\prime}(t)\leq C_{T}\int_{0}^{t}|-\widetilde{V}_{\mu}|^{2}\,ds,\quad t\in[0,T].

By the classic comparison theorem, we have
-\widetilde{V}_{\mu}(t)\leq V(t)
for almost every
t\in[0,T]
, where
V\equiv 0
is the unique solution to the Riccati equation
dV(t)=C_{T}\cdot|V(t)|^{2}
.
In conclusion,

\displaystyle 0\leq\widetilde{V}_{\mu}(t)\leq\frac{\mu([0,t])}{c},\quad t\in[0,T_{\infty}),

which yields that
T_{\infty}=\infty
and
\widetilde{V}_{\mu}
is a
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
-global solution.

Associated to
\widetilde{V}_{\mu}
we define the function
V_{\mu}
as follows

\displaystyle V_{\mu}(t):=W^{\prime}*d\mu(t)-\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ\widetilde{V}_{\mu}\big)*W^{\prime}(t),\quad t\geq 0.

It is obvious that
V_{\mu}(t)=\widetilde{V}_{\mu}(t)
for almost every
t\geq 0
.
The continuity of
\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ\widetilde{V}_{\mu}\big)*W^{\prime}
follows by the regularity property of convolution.
Moreover, by Corollary 6.2 in
[
35
, p.98]
,

\displaystyle W^{\prime}*d\mu(t)=W^{\prime}(0)\cdot\mu\big([0,t]\big)+\int_{[0,t]}\big(W^{\prime}(t-s)-W^{\prime}(0)\big)\mu(ds),

is càdàg in
t
.
Hence
V_{\mu}\in D(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R}_{+})
satisfies (
1.27
) for all
t\geq 0
.

The uniqueness follows directly from Proposition
8.2
and Grönwall’s inequality. Indeed, assume that
V_{\mu}^{(1)}
and
V_{\mu}^{(2)}
are two global solutions in
D(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R}_{+})
to (
1.27
).
For each
T\geq 0
, by Proposition
8.2
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\big|V_{\mu}^{(1)}(t)-V_{\mu}^{(2)}(t)\big|\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}^{(2)}\big)*W^{\prime}(t)\Big|\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\big|V_{\mu}^{(1)}(s)-V_{\mu}^{(2)}(s)\big|ds,

which along with Grönwall’s inequality induces that
V_{\mu}^{(1)}(t)=V_{\mu}^{(2)}(t)
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

\Box

Proof of Theorem
1.10
: Part I.
By Lemma
8.3
, it suffices to prove the existence of
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
-noncontinuable solutions to (
1.27
).
The proof is carried out in the following three steps.

Step 1.
We first prove the existence of local solutions near
0
.
Consider a mapping
\mathcal{R}_{0}
that acts on
f\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
according to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{0}\circ f(t):=W^{\prime}*d\mu(t)-\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ f\big)*W^{\prime}(t),\quad t\geq 0.

For each
T,K\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{B}_{T,K}
be the collection of all functions
f\in L^{\infty}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
with
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}}\leq K
.
It can be easily identify that
\mathcal{B}_{T,K}
is a closed, bounded and convex subset in
L^{\infty}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
.
For any
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{T,K}
, by (
1.7
), Proposition
8.1
and
8.2
there exists a constant
C_{0}>0
such that for any
K\geq 0
and
T\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{0}\circ f_{1}\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}}\leq C_{0}+C_{0}\cdot K^{2}e^{K}\cdot T\quad\mbox{and}\quad\big\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{0}\circ f_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{0}\circ f_{2}\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}}\leq C_{0}\cdot Te^{4K}\cdot\big\|f_{1}-f_{2}\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}}.

Choosing
K_{0}\in(1,1+1/C_{0})
and
T_{0}\in(0,e^{-4K_{0}}]
, we have

\displaystyle C_{0}+C_{0}\cdot K_{0}^{2}e^{K_{0}}\cdot T_{0}\leq K_{0}\quad\mbox{and}\quad C_{0}\cdot T_{0}e^{4K_{0}}<1.

Consequently, the mapping
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{0}
is a contractive from
\mathcal{B}_{T_{0},K_{0}}
to itself.
By Banach’s fixed point theorem, there exists a unique point
V_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{T_{0},K_{0}}
such that

\displaystyle V_{0}(t)=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{0}\circ V_{0}(t)=W^{\prime}*d\mu(t)-\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{0}\big)*W^{\prime}(t),

for almost every
t\in[0,T_{0}]
and hence
V_{0}
is a local solution of (
1.27
) on
[0,T_{0}]
.

Step 2.
We now extend the preceding local solution onto a larger interval.
Denote by
\mathcal{T}
the collection of all
T>0
such that (
1.27
) has a
L^{\infty}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
-local solution.
We assert that
\mathcal{T}
is an open interval containing
[0,T_{0}]
.
Indeed, for any
t_{0}\in\mathcal{T}
and some
k_{0}>0
, assume that
v_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{t_{0},k_{0}}
is a local solution of (
1.27
).
For
t\geq 0
, let

\displaystyle H_{1}(t):=W^{\prime}*d\mu(t_{0}+t)-\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ v_{0}(s)W^{\prime}(t_{0}+t-s)ds.

Similarly as in the proof of Proposition
8.1
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\big|H_{1}(t)\big|\leq\frac{\mu([0,t_{0}+t])}{c}+C.

We consider a mapping
\mathcal{R}_{1}
acting on functions
f\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{1}\circ f(t):=H_{1}(t)-\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ f\big)*W^{\prime}(t),\quad t\geq 0.

From Proposition
8.1
and
8.2
, there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that for any
T\in[0,1]
,
K>0
,
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{T,K}
and
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{1}\circ f_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}}\leq C_{1}+C_{1}\cdot K^{2}e^{K}\cdot T\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{1}\circ f_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{1}\circ f_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}}\leq C_{1}\cdot Te^{4K}\cdot\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}}.

Similarly as in Step 1, we choose
K_{1}\in(1,1+1/C_{1})
and
T_{1}\in(0,e^{-4K_{1}}]
, which induces that

\displaystyle C_{1}+C_{1}\cdot K_{1}^{2}e^{K_{1}}\cdot T_{1}\leq K_{1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad C_{1}\cdot T_{1}e^{4K_{1}}<1.

Hence
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
is a contractive mapping from
\mathcal{B}_{T_{1},K_{1}}
to itself.
By Banach’s fixed point theorem, there exists a unique point
v_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{T_{1},K_{1}}
such that

\displaystyle v_{1}(t)=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{1}\circ v_{1}(t)=H_{1}(t)-\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ v_{1}\big)*W^{\prime}(t),

for almost every
t\in[0,T_{1}]
.
It is easy to identify that the function

\displaystyle V_{1}(t):=v_{0}(t)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{t\in[0,t_{0}]\}}+v_{1}(t-t_{0})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{t\in(t_{0},t_{0}+T_{1}]\}},\quad t\in[0,t_{0}+T_{1}].

is a
L^{\infty}([0,t_{0}+T_{1}];\mathbb{R})
-local solution to (
1.27
) and hence the interval
\mathcal{T}
is open.

Step 3.
We now prove the existence of
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
-noncontinuable solutions.
Let
T_{\infty}:=\sup\mathcal{T}
and
V\in L^{\infty}([0,T_{\infty});\mathbb{R})
be a solution of (
1.27
) on
[0,T_{\infty})
.
To assert that
(V,T_{\infty})
is a
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
-noncontinuable solution to (
1.27
), it remains to identify that
\|V\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{\infty}}}=\infty
if
T_{\infty}<\infty
.
If not, one can repeat Step 2 to find two constants
t_{1}\in(0,1)
and
k_{1}>0
such that the equation (
1.27
) has a local solution in
L^{\infty}([0,T_{\infty}+t_{1}];\mathbb{R})
.
Consequently, we have
T_{\infty}+t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}
, which contradicts to the definition of
T_{\infty}
.

\Box

8.2
Laplace functionals and weak uniqueness

Associated to the two solutions
X_{\zeta}
of (
1.9
) and
V_{\mu}
of (
1.27
), we first introduce in the next proposition an auxiliary process that plays an important role in the following argument.
For convention, we set
V_{\mu}(t)=0
for
t<0
.

Proposition 8.4

For any
T\geq 0
, the random variable

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y_{T}}:=X_{\zeta}*d\mu(T)-\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\big)*X_{\zeta}(T)

(8.4)

is integrable and has the following equivalent representation

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y_{T}}\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot c\cdot V_{\mu}(T)+\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{T-y}^{T}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big)N_{0}(dz,dy)

(8.7)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}V_{\mu}(T-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy).

Proof.
The local boundedness of
V_{\mu}
induces that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
, which together with Theorem
1.8
immediately yields that
\mathbf{E}\big[|\boldsymbol{Y_{T}}|\big]<\infty
.
Plugging (
4.149
) into the right side of (
8.4
),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y_{T}}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathbf{I}_{i}*\big(d\mu-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\big)(T).

Thus it suffices to prove that the preceding four summands equal to the corresponding terms on the right side of (
8.7
) respectively.

\bullet

For
i=1
, by (
1.27
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{1}*\big(d\mu-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\big)(T)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot c\cdot\Big(W^{\prime}*d\mu(T)-(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu})*W^{\prime}(T)\Big)=\zeta\cdot c\cdot V_{\mu}(T).

\bullet

For
i=2
, an application of Fubini’s theorem along with the following two identities

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{T-t-y}^{T-t}W^{\prime}(r)dr\mu(dt)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{y}W^{\prime}(T-t-r)dr\mu(dt)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}\int_{0}^{T}W^{\prime}(T-t-r)\mu(dt)dr

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}\int_{0}^{T-r}W^{\prime}(T-r-t)\mu(dt)dr

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}W^{\prime}*d\mu(T-r)dr

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-t)\int_{t-y}^{t}W^{\prime}(r)dr\,dt\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-t)\int_{0}^{y}W^{\prime}(t-r)dr\,dt

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-t)W^{\prime}(t-r)\,dt\,dr

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\big)*W^{\prime}(T-r)\,dr

induces that

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{2}*\big(d\mu(T)-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\big)(T)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{T-t-y}^{T-t}W^{\prime}(r)dr\Big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)\mu(dt)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-t)\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{t-y}^{t}W^{\prime}(r)dr\Big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)\,dt

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{t-y}^{t}W^{\prime}(r)dr\mu(dt)\Big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-t)\int_{t-y}^{t}W^{\prime}(r)dr\,dt\Big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{y}W^{\prime}*d\mu(T-r)dr\Big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{y}\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\big)*W^{\prime}(T-r)\,dr\Big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{y}\big(d\mu-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\big)*W^{\prime}(T-r)\,dr\,N_{0}(dz,dy)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{y}V_{\mu}(T-r)\,dr\,N_{0}(dz,dy)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{T-y}^{T}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\,N_{0}(dz,dy).

Here the last two equalities follow from (
1.27
) and the change of variables respectively.

\bullet

For
i=3
, by using (
2.8
) along with (
1.7
) and the fact that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{3}*\big(d\mu(T)-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\big)(T)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{T-t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}W^{\prime}(T-t-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)\mu(dt)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-t)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}W^{\prime}(t-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)dt

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{T-s}W^{\prime}(T-s-r)\mu(dr)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{T-s}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s-r)W^{\prime}(r)\,dr\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}V_{\mu}(T-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz).

\bullet

For
i=4
, it is easy to use (
1.10
) to identify that conditions in Proposition
2.6
are satisfied.
By using (
2.9
) along with

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-s}\int_{T-s-t-y}^{T-s-t}W^{\prime}(r)\,dr\mu(dt)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-s}\int_{0}^{y}W^{\prime}(T-s-t-r)\,dr\mu(dt)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}\int_{0}^{T-s}W^{\prime}(T-s-r-t)\mu(dt)\,dr

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}\int_{0}^{T-s-r}W^{\prime}(T-s-r-t)\mu(dt)\,dr

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}W^{\prime}*d\mu(T-s-r)\,dr

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-s}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s-t)\int_{t-y}^{t}W^{\prime}(r)\,dr\,dt\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-s}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s-t)\int_{0}^{y}W^{\prime}(t-r)\,dr\,dt

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}dr\int_{0}^{T-s}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s-t)W^{\prime}(t-r)\,dt

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}dr\int_{0}^{T-s-r}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s-r-t)W^{\prime}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu})*W^{\prime}(T-s-r)\,dr

induces that

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{4}*\big(d\mu(T)-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\big)(T)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{t-s-y}^{t-s}W^{\prime}(r)\,dr\Big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)\mu(dt)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-t)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{t-s-y}^{t-s}W^{\prime}(r)\,dr\Big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)dt

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T-s}\int_{T-s-t-y}^{T-s-t}W^{\prime}(r)\,dr\mu(dt)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T-s}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s-t)\int_{t-y}^{t}W^{\prime}(r)\,dr\,dt\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{y}W^{\prime}*d\mu(T-s-r)\,dr\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{y}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu})*W^{\prime}(T-s-r)\,dr\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{y}V_{\mu}(T-s-r)\,dr\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy).

\Box

Associated to
\boldsymbol{Y_{T}}
we define a Doob’s martingale
Y_{T}:=\big\{Y_{T}(t):t\in[0,T]\big\}
with

\displaystyle Y_{T}(t):=\mathbf{E}\big[\boldsymbol{Y_{T}}\,\big|\,\mathscr{F}_{t}\big].

(8.8)

Conditionally on
\mathscr{F}_{t}
, we take expectations on both sides of (
8.7
) and obtain the following representation for the martingale
Y_{T}
:

\displaystyle Y_{T}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot c\cdot V_{\mu}(T)+\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{T-y}^{T}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big)N_{0}(dz,dy)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}V_{\mu}(T-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

(8.10)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy),\quad t\in[0,T].

Additionally, we consider a
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-process
Z_{T}:=\{Z_{T}(t):t\in[0,T]\}
defined by

\displaystyle Z_{T}(t):=\mathbf{E}\big[X_{\zeta}*d\mu(T)\,\big|\,\mathscr{F}_{t}\big]-\int_{t}^{T}\big(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}\big)(T-s)\mathbf{E}\big[X_{\zeta}(s)\,\big|\,\mathscr{F}_{t}\big]ds.

(8.11)

Lemma 8.5

The process
Z_{T}
is a càdlàg
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-semimartingale with the following decomposition

\displaystyle Z_{T}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle Y_{T}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s)X_{\zeta}(s)ds,\quad t\in[0,T].

(8.12)

Proof.
Plugging (
8.4
) into (
8.8
) induces that

\displaystyle Y_{T}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[X_{\zeta}*d\mu(T)\big|\mathscr{F}_{t}\big]-\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{0}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s)X_{\zeta}(s)\,ds-\int_{t}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{0}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s)\mathbf{E}\big[X_{\zeta}(s)\big|\mathscr{F}_{t}\big]\,ds.

The desired representation (
8.12
) follows immediately by moving the second term on the right-hand side to the left-hand side.

\Box

Note that
Z_{T}(0)=Y_{T}(0)
.
Applying Itô’s formula (see Theorem 5.1 in
[
39
, p.66]
) along with (
8.10
)-(
8.12
) to
e^{-Z_{T}(t)}
and then using (
1.27
), we have

\displaystyle e^{-Z_{T}(t)}\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle e^{-Y_{T}(0)}-\int_{0}^{t}e^{-Z_{T}(s)}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s)X_{\zeta}(s)ds+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-Z_{T}(s)}\cdot c\cdot\big|V_{\mu}(T-s)\big|^{2}\cdot X_{\zeta}(s)ds

(8.16)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-Z_{T}(s)}\cdot\mathcal{V}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s)\cdot X_{\zeta}(s)ds-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}e^{-Z_{T}(s)}\cdot V_{\mu}(T-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-Z_{T}(s-)}\cdot\Big(\exp\Big\{-\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big\}-1\Big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle e^{-Y_{T}(0)}-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}e^{-Z_{T}(s)}\cdot V_{\mu}(T-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

(8.19)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-Z_{T}(s-)}\cdot\Big(\exp\Big\{-\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big\}-1\Big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy).

Consider a process
U_{T}:=\big\{U_{T}(t):t\in[0,T]\big\}
defined by

\displaystyle U_{T}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\exp\Big\{-\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)dr\Big\}-1\Big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}V_{\mu}(T-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz),

which is a
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-martingale and allows us to rewrite (
8.16
) into

\displaystyle e^{-Z_{T}(t)}\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle e^{-Y_{T}(0)}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-Z_{T}(s-)}dU_{T}(s),\quad t\in[0,T],

(8.20)

Multiplying both sides by
e^{Y_{T}(0)}
shows that the process

\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}:=\big\{e^{Y_{T}(0)-Z_{T}(t)}:t\in[0,T]\big\}

is the Doléan-Dade exponential associated to
U_{T}
.

Lemma 8.6

The Doléans-Dade exponential
\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}
is a true
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-martingale.

Proof.
By Itô’s formula; see Theorem 5.1 in
[
39
, p.66]
, the stochastic exponential
\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}
admits the following representation

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\exp\bigg\{-\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\circ V_{\mu}(T-s)X_{\zeta}(s)ds-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}V_{\mu}(T-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

\displaystyle\ \ \quad\quad-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)dr\Big)\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy)\bigg\},\quad t\geq 0.

The non-negativity of
V_{\mu}
induces that all jumps of
U_{T}
are larger than
-1
, which induces that
\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}
is a non-negative local martingale and hence a supermartingale.
By Fatou’s lemma, we have
\mathbf{E}[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}(t)]\leq 1
.
Thus
\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}
is a true
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-martingale if we can identify that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}(t)\big]=1,\quad t\in[0,T].

(8.21)

For each
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and
n\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle\tau_{n}:=\inf\bigg\{r\geq 0:\int_{0}^{r}X_{\zeta}(s)ds\geq n\bigg\}\wedge t_{0}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}(t):=\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}(\tau_{n}\wedge t),\quad t\geq 0.

Here we make the convention that
\inf\emptyset=\infty
.
It is obvious that as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\tau_{n}\overset{\rm a.s.}{\to}t_{0}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}(\cdot)\overset{\rm a.s.}{\to}\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}(t_{0}\wedge\cdot).

(8.22)

The fact that
V_{\mu}\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R}_{+})
induces that there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{n}}X_{\zeta}(s)\big|V_{\mu}(T-s)\big|^{2}\,ds\leq\int_{0}^{\tau_{n}}X_{\zeta}(s)\,ds\cdot\big\|V_{\mu}\big\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{T}}<C\cdot n

Also, by the inequality
|1-(1+z)e^{-z}|\leq|z|^{2}
for any
z\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{n}}X_{\zeta}(s)\,ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg|1-\Big(1+\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big)\exp\Big\{-\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big\}\bigg|\,\nu(dy)

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau_{n}}X_{\zeta}(s)\,ds\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg|\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\bigg|^{2}\,\nu(dy)\leq\int_{0}^{\tau_{n}}X_{\zeta}(s)\,ds\cdot\big\|V_{\mu}\big\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{T}}\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(T\wedge y\big)^{2}\,\nu(dy),

which can be uniformly bounded by
C\cdot n
.
By using Theorem IV.3 in
[
56
]
with

\displaystyle M^{c}_{t}=-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{n}}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}V_{\mu}(T-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

(8.23)

and

\displaystyle y(s,z)=\exp\Big\{-\int_{T-s-z}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)dr\Big\}-1,\quad\nu^{M}_{t}(dz)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\leq\tau_{n}\}}\cdot X_{\zeta}(s)\,ds\,\nu(dz),

the process
\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}
is a uniformly integrable martingale for each
n\geq 1
. Thus

\displaystyle 1=\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}(t_{0})\big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}(t_{0});\tau_{n}=t_{0}\big]+\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}(t_{0});\tau_{n}<t_{0}\big]

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}(t_{0});\tau_{n}=t_{0}\big]+\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}(t_{0});\tau_{n}<t_{0}\big].

By the monotone convergence theorem and the first limit in (
8.22
),

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}(t_{0});\tau_{n}=t_{0}\big]=\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}(t_{0})\big].

Therefore, to obtain (
8.21
) it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}(t_{0});\tau_{n}<t_{0}\big]=0.

(8.24)

Associate with the martingale
\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}
, we define a probability law
\mathbf{Q}^{n}
on
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathscr{F}_{t})
by

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathbf{Q}^{n}}{d\mathbf{P}}=\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}(\tau_{n}).

We now consider the random elements
(X_{\zeta},N_{0},B_{c},\widetilde{N}_{\nu})
under this new probability law.

\bullet

Note that
\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}(0)\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}1
, the Poisson random measure
N_{0}(dz,dy)
is
\mathscr{F}_{0}
-measurable and has the same law under
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}^{n}
.

\bullet

By the classical Girsanov’s Theorem; see Theorem 3.11 in
[
40
, p.168]
, under
\mathbf{Q}^{n}
the continuous martingale
M^{c}
defined by (
8.23
) has predictable quadratic variation

\displaystyle\langle M^{c}\rangle_{t}=2c\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\leq\tau_{n}\}}\cdot X_{\zeta}(s)\cdot\big|V_{\mu}(T-s)\big|^{2}ds,\quad t\geq 0,

and the Gaussian white noise
B_{c}(ds,dz)
has intensity
2c\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{s\leq\tau_{n}\}}\cdot ds\,dz
.

\bullet

By Girsanov’s theorem for random measure; see Theorem 3.17 in
[
40
, p.170]
, the Poisson random measure
N_{\nu}(ds,dy,dz)
is a random point measure under
\mathbf{Q}^{n}
with intensity

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{s\leq\tau_{n}\}}\cdot\exp\Big\{-\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)dr\Big\}\,ds\,dz\,\nu(dy),

\bullet

For each
t_{1}>0
, we consider the auxiliary process

\displaystyle X_{\zeta,t_{1}}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot c\cdot W^{\prime}(t)+\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t)-W(t-y)\big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}W^{\prime}(t_{1}-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t_{1}-s)-W(t_{1}-s-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy),\quad t\geq 0.

It is obvious that
X_{\zeta,t_{1}}
is a
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-semimartingale with
X_{\zeta,t_{1}}(t_{1})\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}X_{\zeta}(t_{1})
under
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}^{n}
.
By Girsanov’s theorem for semimartingales; see Theorem 3.24 in
[
40
, p.172]
, the process
X_{\zeta,t_{1}}
is also a semimartingale under
\mathbf{Q}^{n}
with the following representation

\displaystyle X_{\zeta,t_{1}}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot\big(1-bW(t)\big)+\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t)-W(t-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{0}(dz,dy)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{n}}X_{\zeta}(s)\cdot\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t_{1}-s)-W(t_{1}-s-y)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big(\exp\Big\{-\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big\}-1\Big)\nu(dy)-2c\cdot W^{\prime}(t_{1}-s)\bigg]ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}W^{\prime}(t_{1}-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t_{1}-s)-W(t_{1}-s-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy),\quad t\geq 0.

By setting
t=t_{1}
and then using the arbitrariness of
t_{1}
, the stochastic Volterra equation (
1.9
) under
\mathbf{Q}^{n}
turns to be

\displaystyle X_{\zeta}(t)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot\big(1-bW(t)\big)+\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t)-W(t-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{0}(dz,dy)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{n}}X_{\zeta}(s)\cdot\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t-s)-W(t-s-y)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big(\exp\Big\{-\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big\}-1\Big)\nu(dy)-2c\cdot W^{\prime}(t-s)\bigg]\,ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}W^{\prime}(t-s)\,B_{c}(ds,dz)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{X_{\zeta}(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t-s)-W(t-s-y)\big)\,\widetilde{N}_{\nu}(ds,dz,dy),\quad t\geq 0.

We write
\mathbf{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{n}}
for the expectation under the law
\mathbf{Q}^{n}
.
Taking expectations on both sides of this equation and then using Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{n}}\big[X_{\zeta}(t)\big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot\big(1-bW(t)\big)+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{n}}\mathbf{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{n}}\big[X_{\zeta}(s)\big]\cdot\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(W(t-s)-W(t-s-y)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big(\exp\Big\{-\int_{T-s-y}^{T-s}V_{\mu}(r)\,dr\Big\}-1\Big)\nu(dy)-2c\cdot W^{\prime}(t-s)\bigg]\,ds.

Since
W
is non-decreasing and
V_{\mu}
,
W^{\prime}
are non-negative, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{n}}\big[X_{\zeta}(t)\big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot\big(1-bW(t)\big),\quad t\geq 0.

By the definition of
\tau_{n}
, Chebyshev’s inequality and Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}^{n}(\tau_{n});\tau_{n}<t_{0}\big]=\mathbf{Q}^{n}\big(\tau_{n}<t_{0}\big)\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}^{n}\bigg(\int_{0}^{t_{0}}X_{\zeta}(s)ds\geq n\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{n}}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t_{0}}X_{\zeta}(s)ds\bigg]

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\mathbf{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{n}}\big[X_{\zeta}(s)\big]\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\!

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta}{n}\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\big(1-bW(s)\big)\,ds,

which vanishes as
n\to\infty
.
Hence both (
8.24
) and (
8.21
) hold.
In conclusion, the local martingale
\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}
is a true
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-martingale under
\mathbf{P}
.

\Box

Proof of Theorem
1.10
: Part II.
Note that
e^{-Z_{T}(t)}=e^{-Y_{T}(0)}\cdot\mathcal{E}_{U_{T}}(t)
for
t\in[0,T]
.
By Lemma
8.6
, the process
e^{-Z_{T}}
is a true
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-martingale.
Moreover, by (
8.11
) with
t=T
we have

\displaystyle Z_{T}(T)=X_{\zeta}*d\mu(T).

Taking expectations on both sides of (
8.20
) yields that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\exp\big\{-X_{\zeta}*d\mu(T)\big\}\Big]=\mathbf{E}\big[e^{-Z_{T}(T)}\big]=\mathbf{E}\big[e^{-Y_{T}(0)}\big].

By (
8.10
) with
t=0
and then using the exponential formula of stochastic integral with respect to Poisson random measure; see
[
14
, p.8]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[e^{-Y_{T}(0)}\big]\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\exp\Big\{-\zeta\cdot c\cdot V_{\mu}(T)\Big\}\cdot\mathbf{E}\bigg[\exp\Big\{-\int_{0}^{\zeta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\int_{T-y}^{T}V_{\mu}(r)dr\Big)\,N_{0}(dz,dy)\Big\}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\!\!\!

\displaystyle\exp\bigg\{-\zeta\cdot c\cdot V_{\mu}(T)-\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(1-\exp\Big\{-\int_{T-y}^{T}V_{\mu}(r)dr\Big\}\Big)\bar{\nu}(y)dy\bigg\},

and hence the representation (
1.29
)-(
1.30
) hold.

\Box

Proof of uniqueness of solution to (
1.9
).

Assume that
X_{\zeta}^{(1)}
and
X_{\zeta}^{(2)}
are two solutions of (
1.9
).
For any
T>0
, consider
X_{\zeta}^{(1)}
and
X_{\zeta}^{(2)}
as two
L^{1}([0,T];\mathbb{R}_{+})
-valued random variables.
By (
1.29
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\bigg[\exp\Big\{-\int_{0}^{T}X_{\zeta}^{(1)}(T-s)f(s)ds\Big\}\bigg]=\mathbf{E}\bigg[\exp\Big\{-\int_{0}^{T}X_{\zeta}^{(2)}(T-s)f(s)ds\Big\}\bigg],

for any
f\in L^{\infty}([0,T]:\mathbb{R}_{+})
.
Note that
L^{\infty}([0,T];\mathbb{R}_{+})
is the dual space of
L^{1}([0,T];\mathbb{R}_{+})
, the preceding identity yields that
X_{\zeta}^{(1)}
and
X_{\zeta}^{(2)}
have the same law on
L^{1}([0,T];\mathbb{R}_{+})
and hence on
C([0,T];\mathbb{R}_{+})
. In conclusion, the uniqueness of solution holds for (
1.9
).

\Box

Appendix A
Stochastic integrals driven by
\mathbb{H}^{\#}
-semimartingales

We recall some basic theory of stochastic integrals with respect to infinite-dimensional semimartingales that were firstly studied by Kurtz and Protter
[
45
]
.
Let
\mathbb{H}
be a separable Banach space endowed with a norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{H}}
.
We first recall the definition of
\mathbb{H}^{\#}
-semimartingales.

Definition A.1

We say
Y
is an
\mathbb{H}^{\#}
-semimartingale
if it is
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-adapted and indexed by
\mathbb{H}\times[0,\infty)
such that the following hold

\bullet

For each
h\in\mathbb{H}
, the process
Y(h):=\{Y(h,t):t\geq 0\}
is a càdlàg
\mathbb{R}
-valued
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-semimartingale starting from
0
;

\bullet

For each
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in\mathbb{H}
, and
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
, the following finite additivity holds

\displaystyle Y\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}h_{k},t\bigg)\overset{\rm a.s.}{=}\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}Y\big(h_{k},t\big),\quad t\geq 0.

Let
\mathbb{H}_{0}
be a dense subset of
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathcal{S}_{0}
the collection of
\mathbb{H}
-valued stochastic processes of the form

\displaystyle X(t):=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\xi_{k}(t)\varphi_{k}\quad\mbox{with}\quad\xi_{k}(t):=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\eta_{i}^{k}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{[\tau_{i}^{k},\tau_{i+1}^{k})}(t),

where
m\geq 1
,
\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{m}\in\mathbb{H}_{0}
,
\{\tau_{i}^{k}\}_{i\geq 0}
is a sequence of non-decreasing
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-stopping times and
\eta_{i}^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{i}^{k}}
-measurable.
For any
X\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, define

\displaystyle X_{-}\cdot dY(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{k}(s-)dY(\varphi_{k},t),\quad t\geq 0.

Definition A.2

The
\mathbb{H}^{\#}
-semimartingale
Y
is
standard
if

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{t}:=\Big\{\sup_{s\leq t}|X_{-}\cdot dY(s)|:X\in\mathcal{S}_{0},\,\sup_{s\leq t}\|X(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq 1\Big\}

(A.1)

is stochastically bounded for each
t\geq 0
.

For any
\mathbb{H}
-valued càdlàg process
X
and standard
\mathbb{H}^{\#}
-semimartingale
Y
,
we can find a sequence
\{X^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}\subset\mathcal{S}_{0}
such that as
\epsilon\to 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X^{\epsilon}(t)-X(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\overset{\rm a.s.}{\to}0\quad\mbox{and}\quad X_{-}\cdot dY:=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}X^{\epsilon}_{-}\cdot dY

exists a.s. in the sense that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X_{-}\cdot dY(t)-X^{\epsilon}_{-}\cdot dY(t)|\overset{\rm P}{\to}0
.
Moreover, the limit process
X_{-}\cdot Y
is càdlàg, independent of
\{X^{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
and called the
stochastic integral
of
X
with respect to
Y
.
For any
(\mathscr{F}_{t})
-stopping time
\sigma
, let
X_{-}^{\sigma}(t):=X_{-}(t)\mathbf{1}_{[0,\sigma)}(t)
for
t\geq 0
. We have the following identity

\displaystyle X_{-}\cdot dY(t\wedge\sigma)=X_{-}^{\sigma}\cdot dY(t).

Definition A.3

Consider a sequence of
\mathbb{H}^{\#}
-semimartingales
\{Y_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
.

(1)

It is
uniformly tight
if for each
t\geq 0
, the family
\{\mathcal{H}_{n,t}\}_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly stochastically bounded, where
\mathcal{H}_{n,t}
is defined as in (
A.1
) with
Y
replaced by
Y_{n}
.

(2)

We say
Y_{n}

converges weakly
to
Y
and write
Y_{n}\Rightarrow Y
if for any
m\geq 1
and
f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}\in\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle(Y_{n}(f_{1}),\cdots,Y_{n}(f_{m}))\overset{\rm d}{\to}(Y(f_{1}),\cdots,Y(f_{m}))\quad\mbox{in }D([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{m}).

In addition,
we also write
(X_{n},Y_{n})\Rightarrow(X,Y)
if

\displaystyle(X_{n},Y_{n}(f_{1}),\cdots,Y_{n}(f_{m}))\overset{\rm d}{\to}(X,Y(f_{1}),\cdots,Y(f_{m}))\quad\mbox{in }D([0,\infty),\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}).

Lemma A.4
(Theorem 5.5 in
[
45
]
)

Let
\{Y_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of standard
\mathbb{H}^{\#}
-semimartingales and
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
a sequence of càdlààg,
\mathbb{H}
-valued processes.
If
\{Y_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly tight and

(X_{n},Y_{n})\Rightarrow(X,Y),

then there exists a filtration
\{\mathscr{G}_{t}\}
such that
Y
is an
\{\mathscr{G}_{t}\}
-adapted, standard,
\mathbb{H}^{\#}
-semimartingale,
X
is
\{\mathscr{G}_{t}\}
-adapted, and

\displaystyle(X_{n},Y_{n},X_{n-}\cdot dY_{n})\Rightarrow(X,Y,X_{-}\cdot dY).
